#5148. Brazil Takes Aim at Stablecoins With Shocking Money Laundering Claim

The head of the foreign exchange department at the Central Bank of Brazil has claimed that legitimately obtained money could be considered laundered if it’s used to purchase stablecoins.

The senior Brazilian official claimed that converting money into stablecoins could link it to money laundering, even when it was initially obtained legally.

According to a recent report, Gerson Romantini, who is the director of the foreign exchange department at the Central Bank of Brazil, allegedly made the controversial statements at a private event attended by representatives of financial companies.

Romantini claimed that funds acquired legally could be transformed once they enter the stablecoin ecosystem.

In his view, even legally obtained money could effectively be "dirtied" if used in certain stablecoin schemes, and thus be considered part of a money laundering operation.

It’s important to note that the comments attributed to Romantini do not represent an official statement that all conversions into stablecoins could be considered money laundering.

This statement comes as Brazil has already moved to tighten restrictions on how cryptocurrencies are used for international payments.

On April 30, the Central Bank of Brazil published Resolution No. 561, which took effect on Oct. 1.

The resolution limits the use of virtual assets for the settlement of international payment and transfer services, also known as eFX.

In practice, this means that payments between an eFX provider and its foreign partner must be settled through traditional foreign exchange operations or a Brazilian real account opened by nonresidents.

Crypto payments for these particular settlements are off-limits.

A practice called the "stablecoin sandwich" is particularly concerning.

The way it works is that a Brazilian fintech accepts a certain amount of reais from a customer, then uses this money to purchase stablecoins like USDT or USDC and sends the funds in crypto to an offshore company.

This company, in turn, can convert these stablecoins into fiat currency to make payments or send money to families.

This allows users to make international money transfers for a lower fee and circumvent some of the infrastructure banks use.

The controversial statements have already received a response from Brazil’s crypto industry.

In a statement on Nov. 14, the Brazilian Association of Tokenization and Digital Assets (ABToken) questioned the alleged classification of stablecoin transactions as money laundering.

ABToken maintains that the use of stablecoins is not necessarily illegal.

Furthermore, the association also cautioned that sweeping bans may create legal confusion and hinder legitimate business activities that use stablecoins for international payments.

#5147. Banxa’s Shaun Heng Warns Rapid Fintech Growth Can Conceal Hidden Risks

Home » Interviews » Banxa’s Shaun Heng Warns Rapid Fintech Growth Can Conceal Hidden Risks

An interview with Banxa’s Chief Product & Growth Officer on hidden scaling traps and the future of stablecoin payments.

Rapid growth is often seen as a sign of success in fintech. But as transaction volumes increase, weaknesses in payment infrastructure, fraud prevention and risk management can become harder to spot – and more costly to fix.

Shaun Heng, Chief Product & Growth Officer at Banxa, a global crypto payments infrastructure provider, knows these challenges firsthand. Before joining Banxa, Heng held product and growth leadership roles at MoonPay and CoinMarketCap, gaining experience across two major players in the digital asset space.

In this interview with DailyCoin, he discusses why rapid growth can expose hidden vulnerabilities in fintech, what it takes to build payment infrastructure that works at scale, and how stablecoins could reshape the future of global payments.

– When does rapid growth become a risk for fintech companies?

Our total transaction volume (TTV) grew 76% from January to August. Stablecoins increased by 117%. Great numbers. The first thing I asked was where they came from?

Growth becomes a risk signal the moment it outruns the systems underneath it. That happens more often than people admit.

When volume doubles, every weakness doubles with it. That could mean fraud attempts, chargeback exposure, reconciliation breaks. Just to name a few. So you’re always looking at a great number next to problems that may not have appeared yet.

The second challenge is that fast growth is almost never evenly distributed. It could be one particular partner, region, digital asset, or payment method. That concentration is invisible on a topline chart, so we always look deeper to understand the drivers and future success.

And team culture is real. In a quarter everyone has worked so hard and exceeded targets, nobody wants to be the one asking hard questions.

The discipline I would push for is simple: break down the growth before you celebrate it. Where is it coming from? Which rails is it riding on? And what happens if one of those rails is withdrawn next week?

Growth becomes a risk signal the moment it outruns the systems underneath it.

– What does a payment infrastructure provider need to deliver to make the crypto on-ramp experience seamless?

For a long time the industry shipped widgets, and a widget is a visible admission that someone else is handling this.

Banxa Native is headless by design. The partner owns the interface, the brand, the pricing presentation, the whole journey. We simply sit behind their stack. Users never

Returning users never verify twice, because existing KYC can be reused through token sharing. So, everything the user shouldn’t have to think about. That’s the definition of invisible.

In practice, it means the provider owns licensing, or a licensed local partner, in every market where money actually moves,  local payment methods, not just cards, because users pay the way their country pays.

Vietnam is the example I keep reaching for. We launched VietQR there in Q3, and it is already our top payment method in that region at roughly 42% of all transactions — driving a conversion rate close to eight times higher than cards. Same users, same product, same pricing. The only variable was letting people pay the way they already pay.

Then provider also need identity and KYC, including the ability to accept verification the partner has already done.  Settlement, in and out, with money arriving where it is supposed to on the day it is supposed to.

And it has to run in both directions, not just the buy flow. On-ramp, where a user pays with a local method and receives crypto. Off-ramp, where they sell and cashout to their own bank account.

A lot of products ship the first and treat off-ramp as a secondary problem, and that is precisely the moment a user discovers the money only moves one way.

For a European fintech, Banxa collects the local payment, exchanges and settles the fiat leg where the partner needs it. We carry the MiCA licence that passports across the EU — so they don’t have to build a crypto licensing and payments stack country by country.

One partner, one compliance review, one integration, both directions. That speed to market is usually why partners choose us.

– Banxa’s infrastructure supports stablecoin payments across B2B settlement, consumer payments, and cross-border transactions. What is the most overlooked challenge in making stablecoin payments work at scale?

Nobody has ever been excited about reconciliation, and it is the thing that decides whether you have a business.

A stablecoin transfer settles in seconds. That part is genuinely solved.

What is not solved is everything around it. The fiat leg still moves on banking hours and local scheme rules. You have a bank partner on one side, a chain on the other, and a treasury position sitting between them that has to be funded, hedged and accounted for.

Every transaction has to be matched across those systems, in the right currency, on the right day, with the right fee breakdown, in a form an auditor and a regulator will accept.

The second unglamorous thing is failure handling.

At scale, the interesting work is not the happy path. It is the payment that half-completes.

The most common example is card decline and for many reasons – bank limit, 3D Secure step that timed out etc. In most checkouts that ends the order and the buyer needs to start again, or more often leaves.

Then there are bank transfers that arrive three days late with a truncated reference. The refund that has to go back to a card when the crypto has already left.

Users judge you almost entirely on how those cases are handled, and those cases are where most providers quietly fall over.

We recently shipped Payment Method Switch, which lets the buyer change how they pay without losing the order. It separates the order from the payment, so a buyer can move from card to Google Pay without losing the order or redoing verification, and so they see the new method before confirming.

The third challenge is payment method coverage, which sounds like a commercial checklist and is actually an engineering and licensing dependency.

Supporting the way people pay in the US, Nigeria, Australia and Germany means four different integrations, four different fraud profiles and four different regulatory conversations. A switch only works if the alternative payment method is already connected and live for that buyer.

It matters because this is the layer the user is trusting without knowing it. When the plumbing is right, the experience feels effortless. When it is wrong, it does not feel like a backend issue. It feels like the product lied to them.

– Can KYC be more than compliance — can it actually become a growth advantage in crypto payments?

Yes, and I would go further. KYC is one of the highest-leverage product surfaces in payments. Most teams just never treat it as one.

Look at where users actually drop. It is rarely pricing. It is the verification step.

Someone has decided they want to transact, they have intent, and then they are asked to photograph a document on a screen that does not match the app they were just in. A meaningful share of them never come back.

That is not a compliance cost. That is a conversion problem.

So the advantage comes from three things.

First, verify once. If a partner has already verified a user to a standard we can rely on, that user should not be asked again. Banxa Native supports exactly this through token sharing, so returning users go straight to payment.

Second, verify proportionately. Not every transaction carries the same risk, so it shouldn’t carry the same friction.

Third, verify natively. No redirect, no third-party screen, no moment where the user wonders who they just handed their passport details to.

There is a trust argument too. In most of the world, people are more willing to move money with a platform that looks regulated, not less. Verification done well signals that this is a real financial product. Done badly, suggests the opposite.

My advice is to instrument it. Measure drop-off by step. Iterate. Most teams hand it to engineering once and never look again.

– Crypto payments come with a lot of risk, from payment disputes to fraud. Do you the industry underestimates the value of having someone else take on that risk?

Badly, yes. And the reason is that the cost of carrying it yourself does not show up as a line item until it is already a problem.

The fiat leg is reversible. The crypto leg is not. A card payment can be disputed months after the asset has left. That gap is where fraud lives.

So when a platform decides to run its own ramp, they’re usually pricing the integration work. What they are not pricing is the loss rate, the rules engine that has to keep up with it, the team watching it at three in the morning, the bank relationships that get withdrawn when your chargeback ratio crosses a threshold, and the capital you have to hold against all of it.

Those costs are lumpy and they arrive late.

The real value of transferring that risk is not just the losses avoided. It is focus.

With Banxa Native, the partner owns the experience end to end. We carry the regulated rails and the settlement behind it. They get to spend their engineering time on the things their users actually chose them for.

Risk you have not priced is still risk. Someone is carrying it.

– What’s one thing Web3 companies still see as “just compliance” that should really be treated as a product or growth issue?

Market access. Which licences you hold, and therefore which countries you can actually serve, is usually filed under legal. It is the single biggest determinant of your addressable market.

Teams spend enormous effort on acquisition and then discover the funnel is geographically capped. Users arrive, get to the payment step, and find out their country is not supported or their local payment method is not available.

From the user’s point of view, the product simply does not work. From the growth team’s point of view, the conversion rate is mysteriously bad in half the world. Nobody connects that back to a licensing decision made two years earlier.

The same is true of regulatory readiness ahead of a regime change. MiCA is the clearest recent example.

The companies that treated it as a legal deadline scrambled. The ones that treated it as a product decision kept serving European users without interruption while others paused service. Continuity of access is a growth outcome.

This is also why we think about our licence footprint as product surface area rather than paperwork. Banxa services users across North America, Europe, the UK, Latin America and Asia-Pacific, and is MiCA-authorised as a CASP in the Netherlands. For a partner building on Banxa, that footprint is not a compliance appendix in the contract. It is the list of countries where their product works.

My general rule: if a compliance decision changes who can use your product or how easily, it was never only a compliance decision.

– What development in crypto payments could have the biggest impact on the industry over the next few years?

Stablecoins becoming the default settlement layer for ordinary businesses that have no interest in crypto.

That is the shift. It isn’t necessarily about more people buying digital assets. It’s about companies moving money across borders faster and cheaper, with the stablecoin being an implementation detail they never discuss.

The future isn’t crypto payments. It’s better payments.

Stablecoin supply has actually contracted since May, from around $310 billion to roughly $286 billion. But payment volume went the other way. Allium reported it at $482 billion in the first nine months of this year, up 45% year on year.

Banks, card networks and large fintechs are integrating this into their products right now.

What makes it transformative is the disappearance, not the technology.

Consumers will keep using the apps and currencies they already trust. The rails underneath will change. Identity handled once, not repeatedly. Settlement in minutes instead of days. Fees people can actually see.

In many emerging markets, this will go further and faster than people in Washington or London expect, because the alternative is genuinely slow.

Digital dollars become the practical choice for savings and cross-border flows, not a theoretical one.

Most users will never know a stablecoin was involved. That’s the point.

#5146. Why the ‘Blue Wave’ coming for Trump’s $1.5B crypto empire may be mostly good news

Read the best crypto stories of the day in less than 5 minutes. Join to get it daily in your inbox.

Read the best crypto stories of the day in less than 5 minutes. Join to get it daily in your inbox.

Alaska is yet to be called, but the writing is on the wall for President Donald Trump and the Republican Party. The Democrats have seized control of both the U.S. Senate and the U.S. House of Representatives.

For all intents and purposes, this means that Trump is now a lame duck president.

That’s the least of his worries, though, as Democrats could now set their sights on investigating Trump’s crypto ventures. After all, this is a man who amassed a fortune of close to $1.5 billion from cryptocurrency ventures in the first year of his second term in office.

Donald Trump became the first sitting president to launch a memecoin, with OFFICIAL TRUMP [TRUMP] generating over $600M in licensing fees and royalties alone. All while a majority of the memecoin’s retail investors saw red.

He is also the “Co-Founder Emeritus” of World Liberty Financial [WLFI], a DeFi and cryptocurrency platform. According to public filings, the project, co-founded by Eric Trump, Barron Trump, and Donald Trump Jr., brought in $799M in 2025 alone.

Then there’s the matter of an Abu Dhabi-based fund buying $2 billion in USD1, WLF’s own stablecoin, to invest in Binance after the crypto exchange was fined over $4 billion for money laundering violations. Its founder, Changpeng Zhao, pled guilty and served a 4-month jail term before being pardoned by Trump.

Simply put, it can be argued that the president has used his position (and crypto) to monetize the presidency and enrich his personal fortune. Hence, the investigations.

Moving ahead, what should the industry expect now that the Democrats are in control of the U.S Congress?

Well, it wouldn’t be unreasonable to expect a gridlock. After all, many in the crypto industry have historically backed Republican candidates because they championed free market de-regulation and digital asset frameworks.

On the contrary, several prominent Democrats like Sen. Elizabeth Warren, who is also expected to chair the Senate Banking Committee from January, have often been vocal critics of the sector.

However, expecting the worst outcome possible here would allude to a very superficial reading of the situation. This is why it’s important to differentiate between what will happen, what can happen, and what won’t happen.

Yes, the likes of Warren haven’t been the most receptive towards the sector, but party-wide support has been growing over the years.

Consider this – following the latest midterm elections, there are a total of 50 pro-crypto senators against just 33 anti-crypto ones. Similarly, in the House, 241 are currently pro-crypto as opposed to 104 who are against crypto.

Those are significant numbers. More importantly, they also suggest that despite some historical bias, the sector enjoys some degree of bipartisan support.

The example of Fairshake is a case in point. The Crypto Super PAC spent millions backing 33 House candidates during the midterms. 19 Republicans and 14 Democrats. At the time of writing, 29 of them had already won.

Legislative evidence from before the midterms is worth looking at too. As Andrew Nalichaev, CEO of HAIA Finance, pointed out,

… support for rules concerning stablecoins has received bipartisan backing: the GENIUS Act passed the Senate 68 to 30 and the House 308 to 122. An important issue that led to the failure of the CLARITY Act in September was the ethics issue regarding officials involved in issuing or supporting tokens.

Likewise, Luke Jacobi, Founder and Editor at OptimistFi, told AMBCrypto,

Our small comparison of two House final-passage votes on July 17, 2025 found that 102 of 212 voting Democrats backed GENIUS, compared with 78 of 212 for CLARITY: 48.1% versus 36.8%. Both attracted cross-party support, but the 24-vote difference shows why a single ‘pro-crypto’ label misses policy-specific disagreement.

According to a recent report, over 30% of the population, or 70M people, now hold digital assets. It is evidence that despite some skepticism and vocal criticism, the industry has reached a level of financial integration that will be difficult to reverse right now.

It is also evidence that crypto is no longer a single-party issue. Both red and blue states have their own share of crypto holders. In time, their numbers will grow, and so will the degree of bipartisan support the industry enjoys from lawmakers.

However, the concerns about a possible legislative gridlock and its potential impact on the regulatory clarity the industry desperately seeks aren’t totally unwarranted. In the current landscape, Democrats control the U.S. Congress, while Republicans control the White House.

And while he did suggest that he looks forward to working with Democrats, President Trump has also asserted that he will wield his veto power if need be, especially since Democrats don’t enjoy a veto-proof or filibuster-proof majority.

This means that realistically, we can expect a certain level of gridlock across the board ranging from budgets, appropriations, and defense spending. Even crypto, if the new U.S. Congress is intent on investigating President Trump’s ventures.

Hence, the question: How big a concern could this be? According to Dmitri Mihhailov, General Partner at Prifinance,

Investigations do not change laws already on the books, and the most important one for the industry is already there – The GENIUS Act.

Mihhailov also told AMBCrypto that in his view,

… gridlock hurts what is still missing: a federal framework for crypto trading markets. Until Congress passes one, firms rely on agency guidance and court rulings, and a future SEC or CFTC can change guidance much more easily than a statute.

In fact, it can be argued that it’s not the investigation itself that could be concerning for the crypto-sector. Instead, it’s the calendar that could be the problem. Especially since congressional investigations consume time, staff, resources, and political capital, all of which can be used to pass necessary crypto legislation.

The “Wild West.” That’s the reputation the cryptocurrency industry had once upon a time.

Gone are those days, though, as the sector became more mainstream and was embraced by institutions and people world over. Banks, lawmakers, and even nation-states. Heck, even Trump did that within years of calling Bitcoin “a scam.”

Politics is a fickle game, though. It’s not unreasonable to contemplate that some legislators could turn on the industry if the congressional investigations into Trump’s crypto ventures go underway.

The thing is, as an industry, crypto has well and truly arrived on the world stage over the last few years. Despite perceptions, it is too big to be defined by a singular person’s business interests. In fact, more than political scrutiny, THAT would be the biggest threat to the sector.

The danger is that crypto as a whole becomes politically coded through its association with high-profile ventures like memecoins and politically connected stablecoins. If every crypto vote starts to look like a vote on a specific politician, moderates on both sides will find it easier to abstain or delay.

Then what could these investigations yield? Well, at the very least, clear-cut ethics, disclosures, and conflict of interest rules for public officials – proposals that were at the core of the CLARITY Act’s failure. And, at the end of the day, won’t that be a win for the industry as a whole?

So, no. While there might be some distancing, lawmakers won’t turn on crypto.

2026 hasn’t been a great year for the crypto sector so far. The markets aren’t exactly booming, billions have been lost to hacks, and the CLARITY Act failed to go through. Plus, the current President of the United States could soon find himself at the center of an investigation into his crypto dealings.

It is simply a matter of getting used to the new balance of power in Washington D.C.

#5145. 'A Massive Mistake': Tech, AI and Crypto Founders React to Jensen Huang’s Nvidia Exit

'A Massive Mistake': Tech, AI and Crypto Founders React to Jensen Huang’s Nvidia Exit

Nvidia co-founder Jensen Huang is out as CEO after 33 years, and some of the loudest names in crypto and artificial intelligence think the company just made a spectacular mistake.

On Thursday, Nvidia confirmed Huang’s resignation as president, CEO, and board member, and named longtime finance chief Colette Kress interim CEO. The move followed reports of two extramarital affairs, including one involving a businesswoman U.S. intelligence reportedly suspected of links to China, as well as concerns over a reported relationship with an Nvidia employee.

Huang acknowledged being unfaithful to his wife in a message to employees on Sunday. Nvidia said in a board statement that it had no reason to believe its secrets or national security were compromised, and its independent investigation remains underway. The allegations about foreign intelligence have not established that Huang knowingly provided information to a foreign government.

But even without any leaks, the scandal was enough for the board to decide to let him go.

The market did not welcome the transition. Nvidia shares fell 14% at Thursday’s opening bell; with more than $500 billion wiped from U.S. stocks in a single minute. The company that was once the most valuable in the world has now fallen to third on that list.

Nvidia was already under pressure before the scandal, and Huang’s departure raised a particular problem for a company built so closely around its founder: Who, exactly, can replace him?

Changpeng “CZ” Zhao, the Binance co-founder and one of crypto’s most influential executives, said “it's a mistake for Nvidia to let Jensen resign.” Bitcoin entrepreneur and former Kraken executive Dan Held agreed, writing, “He shouldn't have resigned,” and argued that Huang’s personal problems should be weighed against the company he built.

It's a mistake for Nvidia to let Jensen resign.

— CZ 🔶 BNB (@cz_binance) November 12, 2026

U.S. intelligence officials reportedly assessed Alice Huang as a Chinese intelligence asset, but Nvidia says it has no reason to believe any secrets were compromised, and Singaporean authorities said they lacked sufficient evidence to establish that she committed a crime. As of today, no law enforcement agency has presented any hard evidence against Alice Huang.

Anthony Pompliano, the Bitcoin investor and commentator, moved the conversation from the boardroom to the technology itself. “Do not get distracted by the headlines,” he wrote. “Open-source AI is essential to the freedom of billions of people in the future.”

Open-source AI is essential to the freedom of billions of people in the future.

— Anthony Pompliano 🌪 (@APompliano) November 12, 2026

Surrounding it all is likely the most talked about conspiracy theory in the open-source AI sphere. The ousting was designed to hurt open-source developments, the speculation goes, since Nvidia is one of the leading voices in the open-source movement, which closed-source companies see as a threat.

For Voorhees, the controversy also prompted a practical question. “Is there a decentralized Hugging Face yet?” he asked, alluding to one of Huang’s stated commitments. In other words, the crypto solution to a potential platform bottleneck is to stop trusting a single platform.

That concern is more than a slogan for a founder whose Venice AI business is built around private access to open models. Voorhees previously told Decrypt that the antidote to excessive control over AI was “open-source decentralization.”

Some developers saw a deeper threat in Huang’s departure: the loss of a powerful corporate champion of downloadable AI models, whose underlying parameters, or weights, can be run outside the companies that built them.

The worry is that an Nvidia without Huang could become less willing to confront firms that keep their most advanced models behind proprietary services.

Jun Song, an open-source AI advocate and hardware entrepreneur, alleged that OpenAI and Anthropic had spent millions lobbying Congress to restrict open weights. He pointed to a “Pace the Frontier” campaign and argued that Huang responded by building the Open Secure AI Alliance, ending his post with, “And today, we lost him.”

OpenAI and Anthropic have spent millions lobbying the US Congress to ban open weights.

When they formed the "Pace the Frontier" group to push for strict limits on open models, Jensen stepped up right away.

He created the Open Secure Alliance to fight back.

Look at the list of… pic.twitter.com/OeasotcLO3

In July, Nvidia joined Microsoft, IBM, Hugging Face, and dozens of other organizations to launch the Open Secure AI Alliance, which works on open AI security tools and standards. Nvidia also backed a letter defending open weights against broad restrictions.

The broader conspiracy claim needs, of course, much more evidence. The Pacing the Frontier initiative is a statement from employees at several AI companies seeking tools to slow dangerous frontier development, not proof of a joint corporate campaign to ban all open-weight models or remove Huang.

OpenAI subsequently signed the pro-open-weight letter, while Anthropic CEO Dario Amodei said explicitly that his company had never advocated such a ban.

Other posts on social media showed how much anxiety the debate had generated. A commenter replied to Song that they were now “counting on Huawei and Deepseek for the very survival of the OSS community.”

Huawei and Deepseek are two major Chinese companies, known for their open-source developments

I can't believe now I am counting on Huawei and Deepseek for the very survival of the OSS community. What a loss...

— Look4Newways (@sflk2026) November 12, 2026

The issue comes with a rather large business backdrop. Nvidia agreed in September to acquire the open-source AI repository Hugging Face for roughly $12.9 billion, subject to regulatory clearance, in a deal expected to close in the first half of 2027.

That arrangement could put both the hardware used to build AI and the largest hub used to distribute open models under one company’s influence—arguably, the most powerful company on earth.

Epic Games founder and CEO Tim Sweeney was among the highest-profile tech executives to condemn the exit. “A sad day for NVIDIA, developers, America, and all humanity,” he wrote, praising Huang for keeping innovation moving even as Nvidia grew into an enormous company.

A sad day for NVIDIA, developers, America, and all humanity. Jensen founded and ran one of the few companies to reach a massive scale while driving massive continued innovation and ubiquity, while others fell into the trap of ever-increasing extraction. https://t.co/AkS5XT9HF8

— Tim Sweeney (@TimSweeneyEpic) November 12, 2026

Perplexity AI co-founder and CEO Aravind Srinivas took a more personal approach. “Thank you Jensen Huang for 33 years of bold leadership,” he wrote. He also credited Huang’s generosity toward other founders, including himself, for helping shape the future of American AI.

Peter Steinberger, creator of the open-source OpenClaw agent and now at OpenAI, argued that the decision felt rushed. “If that spy’s goal was to destabilize one of the most successful American companies, they achieved their goal,” he wrote on X. “Ousting Jensen feels rushed and not right.”

If that Spy’s goal was to destabilize one of the most successful American companies, they achieved their goal. Ousting Jensen feels rushed and not right.

— Peter Steinberger (@steipete) November 12, 2026

Financial commentator The Kobeissi Letter had urged the board to consult shareholders before any decision, arguing that there was no obvious replacement and citing research on the costs of ousting successful CEOs. After the announcement, the account called the resignation “the most costly mistake of the AI Revolution.”

Former DWS executive and corporate-governance critic Desiree Fixler invoked directors’ fiduciary duty, saying the board should protect shareholders instead of indulging what she called a media frenzy.

In general, most of the reactions were supportive, separating personal affairs from business. The corporate espionage angle has apparently not swayed tech enthusiasts.

This Jensen Huang thing is so ugly. Guy takes a company from zero to absolute pinnacle, through blood, sweat, and tears, a pure founder’s founder, great products, makes so many people rich beyond their imaginations, champions open free local AI, and then the incumbent modern…

— Neil Woodfine (@nwoodfine) November 12, 2026

Not everyone outside that chorus thought the security questions could be set aside. North Dakota Republican Sen. Kevin Cramer had called for Huang’s exit, warning about the risk of blackmail and saying the internal investigation was insufficient. The concern from Washington has been largely uniform: the possibility of compromising pressure against the leader of a company central to U.S. AI infrastructure.

Meanwhile, President Donald Trump has remained supportive of Huang: “He’s definitely suffering,” he told the press. “I think he’ll be in good shape.”

Speculation about Huang’s next job spread just as quickly as criticism of his last one. Responding to an X user asking him to hire Huang, Elon Musk replied, “That would be an honor.”

Nvidia says its board is searching for a permanent CEO from inside and outside the company, with Kress running the business in the meantime. Its next scheduled earnings report is November 17, the first major financial update investors will receive after Huang’s departure.

#5144. Paraguay’s Cheap Hydropower Is Drawing a New Rival for Bitcoin Miners: AI

Strict editorial policy that focuses on accuracy, relevance, and impartiality

Morbi pretium leo et nisl aliquam mollis. Quisque arcu lorem, ultricies quis pellentesque nec, ullamcorper eu odio.

Paraguay has become an important location for Bitcoin mining because of the cheap hydropower available near the Itaipu dam. Now a different kind of energy-hungry business is bidding for the same electricity: artificial-intelligence data centres. The country is approaching a turning point as special electricity contracts for crypto miners are due to expire at the end of 2027, potentially forcing operators to renegotiate at higher rates or move their equipment.

The question is no longer whether Bitcoin mining can use abundant renewable power. It is which businesses deserve access when that power becomes more valuable to the wider economy.

CoinDesk reported that Paraguay's state utility ANDE expects to collect about $350 million from crypto miners in 2026. Mining firms have benefited from electricity pricing around $44 per megawatt-hour, a level that helped turn the country into a major global mining location. The revenue is significant, but it does not establish that the arrangement will remain the best option once national demand and competing industrial uses rise.

The contracts matter because mining profitability is highly sensitive to electricity costs. A site can possess efficient machines and substantial computing power yet struggle if its price per unit of energy rises while Bitcoin mining rewards and transaction fees fail to keep pace. Operators make investment decisions years ahead, so uncertainty over access after 2027 can affect capital spending today.

ANDE plans to allow its special crypto electricity arrangements to expire on December 31, 2027, according to the report. A group of utility engineers has separately proposed limiting the combined allocation for crypto mining, AI and data centres to 700 megawatts, with electricity allocated through an auction at higher rates. That engineering proposal has not automatically become binding government policy.

One prominent operator, HIVE Digital Technologies, has a substantial portion of its mining capacity in Paraguay. Its disclosures indicate that continued operations at affected facilities depend on renegotiating power arrangements. That is a business risk rather than evidence that every mining machine is already shutting down. The timetable still leaves room for discussions, alternative contracts and changes in project plans.

The growing competition is more than an abstract forecast. A company connected to the proposed Iguazú AI City project has secured a long-term power arrangement, according to the same reporting. AI data centres generally need stable, predictable electricity supply to support hardware, cooling and customer service commitments. Their economics are different from Bitcoin mining, where machines can sometimes be switched off when prices rise.

That difference can be both a disadvantage and an opportunity for miners. AI projects may justify higher tariffs through long-term contracts and investment promises, but miners can potentially provide flexible demand when the grid has surplus power. The outcome will depend on how regulators value reliable revenue, employment, infrastructure investment and system flexibility.

The energy-versus-computing trade-off is unfolding worldwide. NewsBTC recently examined MARA's Bitcoin sales amid an AI data-centre strategy, and Hyperscale Data's evolving approach offers another example of companies reassessing where computing infrastructure generates the best returns.

For Paraguay, the next meaningful developments will be power-tariff decisions, contract renewals and evidence of actual investment by both mining and AI businesses. The state utility must also meet growing domestic electricity demand. Cheap hydropower created the mining boom; competition for the same resource may now determine how much of that industry stays.

#5143. Vitalik Buterin Breaks Silence on New Ethereum (ETH) Update

Disclaimer: The opinions expressed by our writers are their own and do not represent the views of U.Today. The financial and market information provided on U.Today is intended for informational purposes only. U.Today is not liable for any financial losses incurred while trading cryptocurrencies. Conduct your own research by contacting financial experts before making any investment decisions. We believe that all content is accurate as of the date of publication, but certain offers mentioned may no longer be available.

Ethereum co-founder Vitalik Buterin has published a lengthy post on EIP-8037, a change planned for the Glamsterdam upgrade. In his latest post, Buterin wrote that something like EIP-8037 is, unfortunately, the only way for Ethereum to scale safely.

With the update, Ethereum will collect an additional gas fee for the creation of new accounts. Creating a new account would cost more than five times as much gas as it does now, with new storage slots and contracts getting more expensive too.

According to Buterin, execution is easy to scale, but Ethereum's state already grows by around 110 GiB per year, and every node has to keep it.

Unfortunately, it will bring more pressure on existing contracts, for example, the OP Stack bridge. The bridge has a hardcoded gas limit of 40,000, and even a small increase in state gas costs would push its operation above it. Mainnet has at least 13 contracts like that, according to the security firm ChainSecurity.

It's a double-edged sword: from one standpoint, it limits the ability of spammers and scammers to fill the network with junk accounts used by illicit actors; from the other, it creates additional financial pressure.

Glamsterdam being postponed is another pain factor here: considering the poor state of the market and Ethereum's struggle, it's clear that any delays in major updates negatively affect the network outlook. The new date is yet to be confirmed.

Vitalik Buterin has been hinting at the change in gas consumption for ages now. Back in 2024, he called multidimensional gas the hardest problem in his own post on the topic.

Setting strict limits was never a farsighted option, and developers have been told that for years. However, it's yet another question: who's actually to blame? Buterin himself admitted that this rule hasn't been written down or repeated since 2021, so newer developers simply didn't know about it.

He now suggests writing down a list of features that are likely to change, from gas mechanics to slot time.

#5142. Will Aave’s $21M bet on Bitcoin’s lending potential restore DeFi liquidity or create new risk?

Read the best crypto stories of the day in less than 5 minutes. Join to get it daily in your inbox.

Read the best crypto stories of the day in less than 5 minutes. Join to get it daily in your inbox.

One of the main metrics to look at is liquidity.

With over 4% of USDT supply being removed in Q4, worth around $10 billion, there is still not enough dry powder to confirm that the market has hit bottom. In this context, Aave’s decision to allow Bitcoin to be used within DeFi lending becomes a lot more impactful. Naturally, the question becomes — Can the move increase capital inflows by allowing users to use Bitcoin as collateral and therefore increase stablecoin volumes?

In essence, could this be the much needed catalyst for a DeFi resurgence?

In a recent development, Aave [AAVE] has gained DAO approval to add support for using Bitcoin as collateral.

The proposal will allow holders to lock up their BTC to borrow stablecoins. Notably, the proposal was unanimously approved on 9th November. This means Bitcoin holders can gain new liquidity through Aave’s $21 million credit market by borrowing stablecoins using their BTC as collateral.

As the chart below shows, Aave will be allocating $21 million to their credit products to five different stablecoins. The two largest stablecoins will be USDT and USDC which have been allocated $8 million each. These account for almost 76% of the total capacity.

Meanwhile, AAVE’s native stablecoin GHO will be allocated $1 million. The last $4 million will be split evenly between USDG and RLUSD with $2 million allocated to each.

Why does this matter? Well, this allocation shows that Aave is looking to blend both traditional stablecoins and newer assets to provide more options for borrowing. Consequently, this will allow Bitcoin holders to borrow against several stablecoin markets using their BTC as collateral.

As Aave Founder and CEO Stani Kulechov explained,

The Babylon-built Bitcoin-backed market on Aave V4 shows how easily new markets can be launched using the Hub and Spoke model. Native BTC collateral, without wrappers or custodians, unlocks a major new source of liquidity for on-chain borrowing and lending.

Crypto lending is slowing down as borrowers reduce their exposure amid market uncertainty.

According to Galaxy Research, the overall crypto-backed loan market fell by 16.78% during Q2, hitting a total value of $56.16 billion. This is the first time that all major lending sectors have fallen since 2022. DeFi lending also saw a sharp fall, with total active loans decreasing from $47.13 billion to $21.94 billion between April and July. In short, traders have lost interest in borrowing against their assets.

However, Aave has seen an opposite trend, with the platform noting all-time highs in active loans on the Aave V4 platform, increasing by almost 100% in Q2 to $500 million. Against this backdrop, the addition of Bitcoin to the lending market could lend further growth potential.

In essence, by allowing BTC holders to use their asset as collateral, the market could see more capital flowing into DeFi, more collateral on Aave, and more demand for on-chain borrowing.

Aave V4’s Hub-and-Spoke architecture provides an isolated environment in which integrations can deploy both standard and custom Spokes without affecting other assets on the Hub.

The crypto market is facing its worst liquidity crunch since the 2022 bear market.

The overall stablecoin market cap has fallen by about $14 billion over the past month. By 10th November, the total stablecoin market cap was around $290 billion, according to DeFiLlama. In fact, the last two weeks alone saw the most net stablecoin outflows ever in over four years. Over half of this drop came from USDT and a further 20% from USDC.

This pressure can be seen across the DeFi space. In fact, the total DeFi TVL fell to around $76.6 billion on 12th November, with stablecoin market cap sitting at $286.9 billion at press time. However, DeFi TVL also fell by 9.7% week-on-week.

Against this backdrop, Aave’s move could offer a new source of liquidity.

There is one technical distinction worth keeping though. This integration doesn’t automatically mean any new stablecoins will flow into DeFi. Instead, borrowers will have access to liquidity that is already sitting in Aave. For liquidity to grow, the protocol will also need to attract more lenders and fresh deposits.

As per the attached chart, there is still a long way to go before the lending market on Bitcoin hits significant levels.

BTC-backed loans reached $3.58 billion in 2025, representing just 0.24% of Bitcoin’s market cap. So, by supporting Bitcoin as collateral, Aave could help unlock this largely untapped lending demand.

Aave’s move could also expose its lending market to greater risks.

According to AMBCrypto, although this new borrowing opportunity on Aave will create new demand, it can also lead to greater speculation. Users can borrow stablecoins using BTC as collateral and then re-lend them into leveraged crypto positions.

Consider this — Bitcoin Futures Open Interest increased by 12.1% in 19 days up to late-October, despite a 5.3% price decrease in BTC. This indicated that traders were increasing their leveraged exposure.

This continued into November, with Bitfinex reporting that this was the fifth time traders added leverage to their positions while BTC fell. The previous 4 times this occurred, traders executed liquidations of long positions worth over $800 million.

For Aave, the concern is that easier access to Bitcoin credit could add another source of speculation.

Ultimately, the move by Aave to integrate Bitcoin comes at a time where DeFi needs liquidity. However, its impact remains to be seen still. While the integration could entice early lenders and stablecoin deposits, there is a risk that high levels of demand could come from speculation and cause further volatility in the market.

Thus, Aave may unlock a new source of liquidity. However, turning that liquidity into DeFi growth will be the test.

#5141. Major Wealth Advisor With $24 Billion in Assets Discloses XRP ETF Stake

Major U.S. wealth management company EP Wealth Advisors has revealed that it holds an investment in an XRP exchange-traded fund (ETF). This was disclosed in the company's latest 13F filing with the U.S. Securities and Exchange Commission (SEC).

EP Wealth Advisors, a California-based investment advisory firm with about $24.4 billion in securities holdings, has revealed that it held 18,400 shares in the Volatility Shares XRP ETF (XRPI) as of Sept. 30, 2026.

This was disclosed in the official SEC filing that the company submitted to the commission on Nov. 13. The filing showed that the investment was valued at $147,936 at the end of the third quarter.

Furthermore, the filing showed that the total portfolio value reported by EP Wealth Advisors stood at $24,380,879,896. This value comprises 1,603 items in the company's 13F information table.

It's worth noting, however, that this isn't the company's total assets under management. It's the value of its reportable securities holdings. There are also assets that are not included in Form 13F.

Interestingly, this isn't the first time EP Wealth Advisors has held an investment in XRPI. Data from its historical 13F filings shows that in the second quarter of 2026, the wealth management company held 13,425 shares in XRPI.

The recent filing shows that the company has now increased the number of shares it holds in the XRP ETF by 4,975 shares. This represents about a 37% increase in the company's XRPI holdings during the third quarter.

Nevertheless, this amount is very small relative to the company's multibillion-dollar securities portfolio. It represents less than 0.01% of the total value of its reported securities holdings.

It's also worth reiterating that Form 13F is simply a filing that shows institutional holdings at the end of a quarter. It doesn't necessarily represent the investment manager's current holdings.

The Volatility Shares XRP ETF was launched in May 2025. Its ticker symbol is XRPI, and it is currently trading on Nasdaq.

XRPI isn't a spot XRP ETF. This means it doesn't directly hold the cryptocurrency XRP. Rather, it primarily derives its exposure to XRP's price movements through XRP futures contracts.

This ETF essentially provides investors with a way to invest in XRP without actually having to hold or custody the cryptocurrency. The ETF aims to deliver investment returns that correlate with the performance of XRP.

As of Nov. 13, the most recent available data from the fund issuer shows that XRPI's net assets stood at about $77.9 million.

#5140. Illinois Crypto Tax Draws Unlikely Alliance of DOJ, Banks and DeFi

Illinois Crypto Tax Draws Unlikely Alliance of DOJ, Banks and DeFi

An unusually broad coalition, spanning the U.S. Justice Department, Wall Street’s biggest lobby, the national Chamber of Commerce and a who’s-who of crypto firms, has thrown its weight behind a legal challenge to Illinois’ first-of-its-kind tax on digital asset transactions.

The groups filed briefs this month in Illinois state court supporting a lawsuit from the Crypto Council for Innovation and the Blockchain Association, which are asking a judge to strike down the state’s Digital Asset Tax Act and permanently block its enforcement.

The tax imposes a 0.2% levy on the full value of a digital asset every time an Illinois customer trades, transfers, or stores it through a broker, even when there’s no sale, gain, or change in ownership.

Critics have called it among the most punitive crypto taxes in the country, in part because Illinois imposes no comparable tax on dollars, gold, stocks or any other asset, and no other state has anything like it.

On Nov. 6, the Justice Department filed a statement of interest arguing the tax undermines federal policy and U.S. leadership in digital assets, calling it vague and discriminatory for singling out crypto for treatment no other asset faces.

The U.S. Chamber of Commerce and the Securities Industry and Financial Markets Association, which represents hundreds of broker-dealers, banks and asset managers, filed an amicus brief warning the tax reaches well beyond crypto-native platforms to the traditional banks, custodians and advisers increasingly offering digital asset products.

A separate brief filed Nov. 11 brought together crypto’s heavy hitters, led by the DeFi Education Fund, Andreessen Horowitz, Paradigm and Uniswap, and including Aave Labs, Chainlink Labs, Solana Policy Institute, Phantom, Jito Labs, Multicoin Capital and Stand With Crypto.

Their filing argues the law’s definitions are so vague they could stretch to software developers and decentralized protocols that hold no customer funds and have no way to identify users or know where a transaction occurred. The brief noted an estimated 2.64 million Illinoisans hold digital assets, the fifth-highest total of any state.

“This tax is unclear, discriminatory, unconstitutional, and harmful to the future of finance,” CCI CEO Ji Hun Kim and Blockchain Association executive Ashok Pinto said in a joint statement.

The challenge dates to August. As Decrypt reported, Illinois agreed in October to pause the tax until July 1, 2027, buying time for the court fight now underway.

#5139. Project Helix Xbox Console Will Run PC Games: What It Means for Web3

Home » Esports » Project Helix Xbox Console Will Run PC Games: What It Means for Web3

Microsoft VP Jason Ronald details how the next-gen console merges Xbox libraries with PC gaming.

Microsoft has officially clarified its vision for the upcoming Project Helix Xbox console, confirming in the Xbox 25th Anniversary Magazine that the next-generation system is built to run both traditional Xbox libraries and PC games. A hybrid shift could also open new doors for Web3 gaming.

As Microsoft celebrates Xbox’s 25th anniversary, the company has shared fresh architectural details about the Project Helix Xbox console, its upcoming next-generation console. When Microsoft first introduced the project earlier this year, it confirmed that the new system would bring Xbox and PC gaming closer together. This led many to assume that Helix would essentially be a Windows gaming PC packaged in a console design.

In a four-page Q&A inside the Xbox 25th Anniversary Magazine, Vice President of Next Generation Jason Ronald pushed back directly against that framing.

“Project Helix is designed to be a console capable of playing games from your PC,” Ronald stated. “It is not a PC that can also play console games.”

For Microsoft, the distinction comes down to removing the friction traditionally associated with PC gaming, such as driver management, multiple launchers, and complex configuration menus, while keeping deeper controls accessible for enthusiasts.

“The goal is making it easy, intuitive, and instant to access my games,” Ronald explained. “But if you’re a hardcore gamer or a power user who wants to have that deeper level of customizability across settings and performance options, you can absolutely go do it.”

According to Ronald and the anniversary feature, Project Helix is being built around several core pillars:

Project Helix, which Xbox CEO Asha Sharma has previously described as a broader “family of devices” rather than a single box, is taking shape during a turbulent stretch for Microsoft’s gaming division.

After taking the helm earlier in 2026, Sharma oversaw major restructuring, studio closures, and layoffs, candidly remarking in July that the Xbox brand was “not healthy.” Simultaneously, ongoing global memory and storage component shortages have pushed up manufacturing costs across the hardware industry, prompting some players to urge both Microsoft and Sony to delay next-gen launches until prices stabilize.

Ronald acknowledged those supply-chain headwinds, calling the current hardware environment “brutal,” though he argued that the constraints are forcing Xbox engineers to innovate rather than simply throw costlier components at the machine.

Microsoft has not announced a price or consumer launch window for Project Helix, though alpha developer hardware is slated to begin shipping in 2027, with retail speculation pointing toward late 2027 or 2028.

For the Web3 gaming sector, Microsoft’s hybrid console-PC strategy could solve a long-standing distribution bottleneck.

Historically, console manufacturers have enforced strict platform policies against blockchain integrations, external crypto wallets, and NFTs, forcing Web3 studios to build almost exclusively for PC, web browsers, and mobile devices.

Still, Ronald noted that the ROG Xbox Ally served as a testing ground for unifying game libraries across multiple stores.

If Project Helix eventually supports third-party PC storefronts such as Steam and the Epic Games Store, it could create new opportunities for blockchain-enabled games to reach console audiences.

However, important questions remain unanswered. Microsoft has not confirmed third-party storefront support on Project Helix, nor has it announced native support for external crypto wallets, NFT functionality, or blockchain transactions.

Nevertheless, the direction is worth watching. If Project Helix successfully bridges the console and PC gaming ecosystems, it could create new distribution opportunities for Web3 games and help bring blockchain-powered gaming experiences closer to mainstream players.

#5138. How $10,000 in stablecoins would have gained €420 without earning interest

A fixed dollar-token balance would have gained 4.9% in euros from September 1 to November 13 before costs. A stronger euro can reverse that effect.

A dollar stablecoin can keep its peg while the euro spending value of a European saver’s balance moves sharply. At a perfect $1 peg, an unchanged holding would have gained about 4.9% in euros between September 1 and November 13, 2026, using European Central Bank reference rates and excluding costs.

The ECB’s household stablecoin analysis puts that currency exposure in context. Its experimental 2025 survey evidence identifies ownership of dollar tokens among euro area consumers. The subsequent exchange-rate comparison illustrates a separate point: stability against the dollar does not promise stability against the currency used to pay the rent.

The bulletin draws on Consumer Expectations Survey modules from August and November 2025 across the 11 largest euro area countries. In August, 5% of consumers reported stablecoin ownership by themselves or household members, while 13% reported crypto ownership.

Among respondents with crypto holdings, 17% reported USDT, 12% USDC and 10% the euro-denominated EURC. Those percentages measure how common ownership was among crypto-holding respondents. They reveal no token’s share of invested capital. Respondents could select several assets, and the crypto-holder subsample comprised 1,286 people.

The ownership observations date to 2025. The ECB flags small samples, questionnaire sensitivity and possible misreporting. It also says the survey does not directly report how assets were used, so the figures cannot establish payment adoption or tell us what households earned.

The two-date illustration takes September 1 as its chosen baseline. Different holding periods can produce different currency returns. The ECB’s dollar reference-rate history shows $1.1590 per euro on September 1 and $1.1052 on November 13, the latest working-day observation available for this comparison.

With fewer dollars needed to buy one euro, the same dollar balance translated into more euros. For 10,000 unchanged tokens worth exactly $1 each, the illustrative value rose from €8,628 to €9,048, a gain of about €420 before costs. Dividing 1.1590 by 1.1052 and subtracting one gives a 4.9% increase.

That entire illustrated gain comes from currency movement. Token quantity and dollar price are held constant, with no interest earned. Actual households’ returns would depend on their holdings, timing and costs. The ECB also publishes its rates for information and discourages transactional use, so retail conversion quotes can differ from these translation values.

The November 13 rate translates the same dollars into about €420 more than the September 1 rate, before costs.

The euro value rises 4.87% in this chosen interval through currency translation alone, before costs.

Hypothetical unchanged balance and exact $1 peg, with no yield, fees, spreads or taxes. ECB rates are informational, not executable quotes. The chosen two-date window is not a household holding period or an estimate of survey respondents’ profits. No APY comparison or forecast.

More generally, gross euro value equals token quantity multiplied by the token’s dollar price, divided by dollars per euro. Income can change the quantity or value of a position; a depeg changes the dollar price; exchange rates change its euro translation. Conversion costs reduce what is available to spend. A stronger euro can reverse the currency effect even if the dollar peg remains intact.

A separate stablecoin-yield study by DefiLlama Research, published November 12, examined a November 9 snapshot of 98 stablecoin pools with at least $50 million supplied, no token incentives over the preceding 30 days and a recorded 30-day base annual percentage yield (APY).

It reported a median base APY of 4.1%. Forty-eight pools exceeded the study’s 4.2% three-month Treasury benchmark, dated November 5; that selected group’s median was 5.4%.

These are annualized yield measures based on a trailing 30-day window. They cannot be compared as realized returns with the September-to-November currency gain. They do show why a quoted income rate gives only part of the euro holder’s picture. DefiLlama links higher premiums to additional credit, trading, liquidity or underlying-asset risks, alongside the risks of using smart contracts.

Bare token ownership is different again. Circle’s European Economic Area (EEA) USDC white paper says USDC itself generates no interest for holders and gives them no entitlement to reserve income. Earning a DeFi yield requires a separate position with its own terms and risks.

Redemption also needs a currency and an access route. Circle describes USDC as redeemable one-for-one for dollars. Although Circle Mint is institutional, eligible EEA retail holders have a separate route through Circle France under its MiCA redemption policy, subject to identity and transaction checks and an EEA bank account able to receive USD. Third-party bank conversion or incoming-payment fees may apply.

A holder saving for euro expenses therefore carries a currency exposure alongside peg and investment risks. Someone setting aside tokens for a dollar expense or offsetting a dollar liability may have a different net exposure. The relevant question is what the balance will buy in the currency the holder actually needs.

#5137. Ripple’s David Schwartz Rejects ‘Role Model’ Label After Cutting XRP Holdings by 88%

Disclaimer: The opinions expressed by our writers are their own and do not represent the views of U.Today. The financial and market information provided on U.Today is intended for informational purposes only. U.Today is not liable for any financial losses incurred while trading cryptocurrencies. Conduct your own research by contacting financial experts before making any investment decisions. We believe that all content is accurate as of the date of publication, but certain offers mentioned may no longer be available.

Ripple CTO emeritus David Schwartz has replied to XRP holders who criticized him for selling most of his XRP with a Saturday Night Live GIF of Natalie Portman saying, "I never said I was a role model."

According to Schwartz, his XRP holdings are down 88%, from a peak of roughly 26 million tokens to 3 million. His Bitcoin went from about 1,500 coins to one, and his Ether went from about 40,000 to six.

Schwartz wrote that he "really doesn't like risk." According to Schwartz, he and his wife agreed years ago to sell some of their coins every time crypto reached a new all-time high. In 2025, Schwartz sold around 2.5 million XRP and paid about $1.7 million in capital gains tax on the sale. He had received these tokens in 2020 and 2023.

@hllywd22 @tchdwntommyd @WHTBOYRICKY @CryptoTaxFixer When XRP hit 10 cents, that seemed ridiculous. When ETH hit $1, that seemed ridiculous. What BTC hit $200, that seemed ridiculous.

Schwartz said that all of his sales were made under written trading plans, and a lawyer approved each plan at least 30 days before each sale. According to Schwartz, his largest risk asset is still his stake in Ripple.

Schwartz also said that he was a skeptic from the start and did not believe in the early price records of crypto. "When XRP hit 10 cents, that seemed ridiculous. When ETH hit $1, that seemed ridiculous," he wrote.

In his view, the price of XRP today depends on overall sentiment in the crypto market, and fees on the network barely move it. Schwartz allows that the token could capture some of the value of a successful ecosystem in the future, but there are no guarantees. On Monday, the price of XRP stands at $0.956.

On Nov. 16, Schwartz also posted a review of the XRPL codebase by the Qwen AI model. "I asked Qwen to rate my work on the XRPL software. It was mostly good. But this was harsh and it's not wrong," he wrote.

According to the review, the overall architecture of XRPL is good. Still, Qwen said that some components of the code are too tightly coupled, and the migration to a new testing framework stopped halfway. The AI model also called the code generation in XRPL opaque.

Several XRPL developers replied to the post and said that they are already rewriting some of these parts of the code. Other users asked for formal verification of the XRPL core before the new features planned for 2027 go live.

#5136. 64K Bitcoin Miners Hijacked: Why the Attack Failed to Shake BTC

Home » Bitcoin (BTC) » 64K Bitcoin Miners Hijacked: Why the Attack Failed to Shake BTC

An AI-assisted exploit of Luxor’s software briefly diverted 1.4% of Bitcoin’s global computing power.

An AI-assisted exploit of Luxor’s fleet-management software briefly diverted 1.4% of Bitcoin’s global computing power, netting the attacker over $10,500 while leaving the blockchain itself untouched.

A coordinated weekend cyberattack hijacked approximately 64,000 Bitcoin mining machines, temporarily redirecting up to 13 exahashes per second (EH/s) of computing power to an unauthorized address on the OCEAN mining pool.

While the incident exposed a major vulnerability in third-party mining management tools, the Bitcoin protocol itself was never compromised, and the diverted hashrate fell far short of the threshold needed to disrupt the blockchain.

Bitcoin mining hosting provider CoreX Hosting was the first to flag the suspicious activity on November 14, warning on X that thousands of machines were suddenly connecting to the OCEAN mining pool under a single external payout address.

Just a heads up, there is a big miner hack going on as we speak. Miners are being pointed at @ocean_mining with BTC address bc1qphukp9rjfc4rcej7erpm9ae20xmtspw8pcddrr .

Less than an hour later, approximately 13 EH/s of computing power, equivalent to about 1.4% of the entire Bitcoin network’s global hashrate, was pointing toward the attacker’s wallet. CoreX said it was working to restore the affected machines’ original mining configurations.

By 1:40 a.m. ET on November 15, CoreX confirmed the attack had been contained. The hashrate tied to the rogue address plunged from 13 EH/s down to roughly 100 petahashes per second (PH/s), signaling that nearly all compromised rigs had been recovered or disconnected.

Initially, blockchain trackers noted that the attacker had failed to mine a single block during the brief hijack window, suggesting the heist had yielded zero profit. However, because of how OCEAN’s payout model works, the attacker still managed to walk away with a modest payday.

Under OCEAN’s reward distribution rules, valid mining shares submitted during an active window remain eligible for payouts when the pool subsequently finds blocks.

On-chain tracking later confirmed that the attacker’s address received payouts totaling between 0.162 BTC (roughly $10,500) and 0.243 BTC across two to three block rewards before the eligible work window expired.

While the attacker’s haul was relatively small compared to the massive computing power hijacked, affected mining operators lost real-time revenue during the outage while continuing to pay electricity costs.

The incident took another turn on November 15, when mining services company Luxor confirmed that its Commander mining equipment management software had been exploited to redirect hashrate.

In a post on X, Luxor said its internal monitoring detected unauthorized pool reassignments affecting “just over a dozen” client accounts. Engineers patched the vulnerability within two hours and began reverting customer rigs to their original pools. Luxor emphasized that no user data was leaked and that its core mining pool and LuxOS firmware remained unaffected.

Last night our internal monitoring alerted us to an unauthorized redirect of hashrate through Commander. Our on-call engineering team jumped on it right away.Impact: just over a dozen client accounts. Affected machines were pointed to a different pool for a limited window. No…

In a follow-up statement, Luxor CEO Nick Hansen apologized to affected clients and said the attack involved a “sophisticated AI-enabled vector.”

He warned that AI-powered cyberattacks are becoming an increasing threat to crypto infrastructure, with automated systems probing for vulnerabilities around the clock.

Luxor promised to fully reimburse affected clients for lost mining revenue, paying them as if their machines had continued mining normally.

The incident exposes a vulnerability in Bitcoin’s mining infrastructure that exists outside the blockchain itself. Still, it never posed a systemic threat to Bitcoin: controlling the network requires over 50% of global computing power.

At its peak, the hijacked hashrate accounted for just ~1.4% of the network and lasted only a few hours—nowhere near enough to alter transactions or double-spend coins.

Because no user wallets were drained and Bitcoin’s consensus rules operated normally, the breach had no direct impact on Bitcoin’s market price, which hovered near the $64,500–$65,000 range amid broader macro-driven market volatility.

#5134. Why Avalanche stood out as demand for other altcoin ETFs weakened

Read the best crypto stories of the day in less than 5 minutes. Join to get it daily in your inbox.

Read the best crypto stories of the day in less than 5 minutes. Join to get it daily in your inbox.

Avalanche [AVAX] is an outlier among other altcoins when comparing their ETF demand. Last week, it was the only altcoin that recorded positive net inflows among the 15 tracked US spot crypto ETF groups.

On the 13th of November, AVAX ETFs issued by Bitwise, VanEck, and Grayscale recorded net inflows of $227K. This brought the cumulative net inflows to $30.59 million, with the total assets across the ETFs at $46.28 million.

This reading implies that institutions are banking on AVAX to outperform other altcoins.

Despite that, the price of Avalanche crypto was falling. This could be attributed to the fact that the inflows were very small compared to what was recorded in early October. For instance, over $2.52 million in AVAX ETFs were bought on the 8th of October.

Over the past week, the price action has plunged from $11.63 to $8.66 but has pulled back to $9.14.

The Bollinger Bands were opening up while the altcoin was in a downtrend but have since started to squeeze as AVAX traded above $9. This indicates either bulls or bears are preparing for a move.

The MACD, which is green, shows that bulls have lost strength, with the size of the bars decreasing and becoming faint.

Therefore, there is a likelihood that the downtrend may be extended, considering the general crypto market is weak. Thus, the consolidation around $9 could be a new lower high (LH) before bears attempt to challenge the previous lower low at $8.59.

But there is also a possibility that bulls could start a recovery at this point, considering AVAX ETFs were seeing positive net inflows. That would place $10 as the next bullish target.

Meanwhile, other altcoin ETFs were seeing net outflows, including Chainlink [LINK], Polkadot [DOT], Ethereum [ETH], and XRP, among others.

For instance, the Chainlink ETF issued by Grayscale recorded $705.59K in outflows on the 13th of November. These outflows by LINK follow a two-month record of strong inflows between August and October.

Similarly, the DOT ETF, which is issued by 21Shares, saw an outflow of $99.35K, reflecting the weakening demand. The price of DOT has declined below $1 over the past two weeks, currently trading at $0.72.

Even so, it remains unclear if AVAX ETF demand can withstand continued market weakness.

#5133. Tether wants its gold token to rival ETFs

New institutional trading and borrowing routes put Tether Gold closer to traditional finance, but access and cash-out terms still matter.

Anchorage Digital’s support for Tether Gold gives eligible institutional clients routes to sell or borrow against XAUT as Paolo Ardoino promotes it as a gold-ETF challenger. The competitive test is how readily those routes deliver funds at the size and price a holder needs.

In Tether’s November 12 announcement, its CEO said he believes XAUT is on track to become “the first true competitor to the gold ETF market.” A day earlier, Anchorage announced custody, principal-desk trading and collateral support for the gold-backed token. Like gold-trust shareholders, token holders still depend on counterparties to turn that exposure into funds.

Tether said KPMG U.S. audited issuer TG Commodities S.A. de C.V.’s financial statements for the year ended December 31, 2025, issuing an unqualified opinion. The announced audit concerns that entity and year, rather than all current Tether reserves or today’s trading and financing terms.

For eligible clients, desk-sale proceeds come from the trading counterparty. Anchorage advertises USD or USDT borrowing against bank-custodied XAUT through arrangements with other clients. Borrowing preserves gold exposure but creates debt; the announcement does not establish bank-funded lending. It also specifies neither a universal sale settlement currency nor a fiat-withdrawal timetable.

Anchorage says it holds XAUT at Anchorage Digital Bank in the US or Anchorage Digital Singapore. The group’s footer names Anchorage Hold LLC as digital-asset trading provider, Anchorage Digital NY LLC for New York agency trading and A1 Ltd. as a principal-trading business. It does not identify one universal trade-contract entity.

Access is institutional, with limited high-net-worth availability and further requirements for certain trading. Custodied tokens lack Anchorage guarantees and FDIC or SIPC protection. Custody therefore provides neither an insured gold deposit nor assured liquidity.

Anchorage’s notice says US persons cannot purchase or redeem XAUT with TG Commodities. The issuer’s terms contain a qualification: US counterparties qualifying as eligible contract participants, a regulatory category, may be accepted at Tether Gold’s sole discretion. Direct purchases and redemptions also require verified issuer-customer status.

Platform eligibility, issuer eligibility and issuer acceptance are separate gates. Neither institutional access at Anchorage nor eligible-contract-participant status alone establishes the issuer’s acceptance. Anchorage’s notice addresses issuer transactions, rather than a blanket ban on permitted secondary trading or custody.

Even an eligible, verified issuer customer does not have an unconditional cash buyback. The terms describe whole-bar redemption for delivery in Switzerland. Instead of taking delivery, a customer can request that Tether Gold attempt a sale to a gold dealer or broker. Only a successful sale produces fiat proceeds, less fees; Tether Gold has no obligation to repurchase the tokens or bullion. That route also needs a buyer.

The iShares Gold Trust product page describes IAU shares trading on NYSE Arca through brokerage accounts. For an ordinary share sale, a market buyer supplies the money through the brokerage transaction. The trust is not paying the retail holder cash for redeeming a share.

Its currently linked prospectus, dated December 22, 2022, reserves creation and redemption of baskets to authorized participants, with gold exchanged for shares. Retail sale access and direct redemption access are different rights in this structure as well.

Borrowing without selling is also available within conventional brokerage arrangements, subject to approval. Fidelity’s margin FAQ lists ETFs held for at least 30 days as eligible collateral, with special security and account requirements, and says checkwriting, bank cards and bill payments can create or increase margin debt. That general policy establishes neither IAU-specific approval nor a particular borrowing limit.

Both structures need a counterparty. Switch between selling and borrowing to follow the funds.

The trading counterparty or market buyer supplies sale proceeds. The desk or broker provides the route.

Another bank client supplies XAUT financing; a brokerage lender may supply approved margin credit. Both create debt.

A sale needs a buyer; a loan needs a lender. Custody alone guarantees neither.

Anchorage notice excludes US direct issuer transactions; issuer terms allow discretionarily accepted US ECPs after verification. Neither custody nor ECP status establishes issuer acceptance. IAU trust redemption is AP-only for gold. Fidelity policy does not establish IAU-specific credit. Sale currencies, fiat withdrawal timing and loan terms remain client-specific.

Qualitative cash-direction diagram from the reported service mechanisms. Arrows show direction, not volume, speed or guaranteed availability. The borrowing panel compares client financing with a general brokerage margin route.

CryptoSlate’s July analysis of XAUT lending already examined the comparison with brokerage margin and restricted gold-trust redemption. Anchorage’s offering extends the institutional service set; collateral utility alone does not make tokenized gold unique.

The measurable comparison remains uneven. iShares reported 9,023,902 IAU shares traded on November 13 and a 0.01% 30-day median bid/ask spread as of that date. Anchorage calls XAUT its most active tokenized-gold product, but its announcement supplies no comparable depth series, quoted spreads or executed lending totals.

Ardoino’s ETF challenge ultimately turns on usable access: a client who qualifies, a counterparty willing to transact and terms that deliver the funds required. Custody and collateral support create routes. Their availability at the needed size and price is the next test.
