#5105. House Oversight Chair calls for possible ban on government officials’ prediction market bets over insider trading concerns

Looking for institutional-grade crypto trading? Discover LMAX Digital, the regulated exchange from LMAX Group

On Friday, the committee released its findings of an investigation it launched in May on insider trading risks in prediction markets among government officials.

The Republican chair of the House Oversight Committee is calling for new limits on government officials' use of prediction markets, including the possibility of an outright ban, amid growing concerns that public officials could use insider information to profit from bets on the platforms.

On Friday, the committee released findings from an investigation it launched in May into insider trading risks in prediction markets among government officials, military personnel, and those with security clearances. The committee sifted through 1,700 pages of documents and also received five bipartisan staff briefings from prediction market companies, said Chair James Comer, R-Ky.

Concerns in Washington have intensified over the past year, particularly after an active-duty U.S. Army soldier who used confidential information to place bets on Polymarket ahead of former Venezuelan President Nicolás Maduro's capture. Thereafter, others in government have also been found using inside information to make money on bets on prediction markets, including the White House's former teleprompter operator. Political candidates have also faced allegations of insider trading on the platforms.

"Congress must consider additional reforms to prevent public corruption and restore the American people's trust in government," Comer said on Friday.

Those reforms should be legislation that restricts which bets federal employees, lawmakers and congressional staff can place on prediction markets — or an outright ban, the committee said.

"Whether it's an outright prohibition on trading on prediction markets or restrictions on some subset of contracts like government-related contracts, applying these restrictions to officials in all branches of government would strengthen ethical expectations that government personnel should not personally and financially benefit from access to non-public information associated with their work as government servants…" according to the memo.

Congress should also have a role in encouraging prediction market companies and federal agencies to share necessary information to enforce restrictions, the committee said.

Both major prediction market companies — Kalshi and Polymarket — have said they have taken steps to tighten controls to prevent government officials from using inside information. Lawmakers have also begun addressing the issue themselves. In April, the Senate voted to pass a resolution to ban members from trading on prediction markets.

#5104. North Korea Stole $400 Million in Crypto Using Deepfake Video Calls, DOJ Says

North Korea Stole $400 Million in Crypto Using Deepfake Video Calls, DOJ Says

North Korean hackers have stolen more than $400 million in cryptocurrency over the past 18 months using a social-engineering scheme built on secretly recorded footage and AI deepfakes, the U.S. Justice Department said Thursday, as it announced the seizure of the infrastructure behind the operation.

The scheme, which the FBI calls “FaceStealer,” turns video conference calls into a trap. According to the DOJ, hackers lure targets into meetings using accounts stolen from previous victims, then play prerecorded webcam footage of trusted contacts to simulate their presence and win the target’s confidence.

The group also generates deepfake videos of well-known figures in the crypto and related industries, using AI to make the ruse more convincing.

Once a target is at ease, the attackers manufacture a fake technical glitch, such as claiming the person can’t be heard, and offer a link to “fix” it, which instead installs malware.

From there, the hackers can drain crypto wallets or exploit the victim’s access to other systems, sometimes waiting months or more than a year before striking. The FBI said it seized over 900 webcam recordings of FaceStealer victims under a federal warrant.

The DOJ tied the operation to North Korean groups known variously as Sapphire Sleet, BlueNoroff and APT38, among others, and warned the hackers have increasingly targeted open-source software developers in an apparent bid to enable supply-chain attacks.

The announcement came paired with an update on the record-breaking Binance hack. The department said it has now obtained court authorization to seize or restrain more than $1.2 billion of the roughly $6.4 billion stolen from the exchange on Oct. 29, part of over $2.7 billion frozen so far.

The FBI attributed that theft to the North Korean group TraderTraitor, and the regime has been laundering the proceeds at speed, spinning up a new wallet roughly every 25 seconds. North Korea has denied involvement, calling the FBI’s finding a political provocation.

The actions underscore how central crypto theft has become to Pyongyang’s finances. The U.S. recently sanctioned 11 people over a North Korean IT-worker scheme that moved millions in crypto, and researchers have cataloged the many ways the regime’s hackers break into crypto firms.

Officials said efforts to trace and recover the stolen Binance funds are ongoing.

#5101. Bitcoin Price Falls 14% in a Week to $65,000 as Weekend Selling Breaks Friday’s Low

Bitcoin price crashed about 13% over the past week and traded near $65,600 on Saturday.

Bitcoin price crashed about 14% over the past week and was trading near $65,200 on Saturday morning after sliding to about $65,000, according to Bitcoin Magazine Pro data. The move broke below Friday’s low of about $67,555 and marked the weakest level since mid-August, as weekend trading extended a selloff that has followed a stronger dollar and a fully priced December Federal Reserve rate hike.

A week ago, on Nov. 7, bitcoin was trading near $75,750. The decline steepened after Nvidia chief executive Jensen Huang resigned on Thursday, a shake-up that caused AI stocks to sell off which could potentially be responsible for sending bitcoin below $72,000. It slipped toward $71,000 by Thursday evening, then crashed to about $67,555 around midnight Eastern on Friday, its lowest level since August, before recovering toward $69,800 in the morning.

A brief pop above $70,000 after the producer-price report faded, and the bitcoin price spent Friday afternoon pinned below $70,000, near $68,900.

Selling resumed Friday evening and carried into the mid-$66,000s. Bitcoin briefly steadied overnight near $67,000, then sold off again after 8 a.m. ET Saturday, reaching that $65,500 low in the 10 a.m. hour and taking out Friday’s trough.

The Labor Department said Friday that the Producer Price Index for final demand rose 0.3% in October, short of the 0.4% increase economists had expected. On an unadjusted basis, the index was up 6.5% from a year earlier, the largest 12-month rise since November 2022. The slightly cooler monthly figure lifted bitcoin above $70,000 for only minutes.

Bond traders were unconvinced. The 10-year Treasury yield climbed as high as 5.17% on Friday, a four-week high, and closed at 5.15%. The U.S. dollar index held at a 19-month high, its strongest level since April 2025. Boston Fed President Susan Collins said Friday that “soon, some additional tightening of Fed monetary policy may be appropriate,” and San Francisco Fed President Mary Daly said policymakers will, “in all likelihood, raise interest rates.” Higher rates raise the cost of holding an asset that pays no interest, and a stronger dollar has weighed on the bitcoin price for much of November. Traders fully priced a quarter-point hike at the Fed’s Dec. 8-9 meeting, CME FedWatch data show.

Chip stocks offered no cushion. The iShares Semiconductor ETF fell more than 3% on Friday, and Advanced Micro Devices dropped more than 8%. The Nasdaq 100 closed down 1.22%. U.S. stock and bond markets are closed Saturday, but those levels still frame the weekend session. Nvidia is due to report third-quarter results on Tuesday under interim CEO Colette Kress, the first earnings release since Huang stepped down.

Demand from spot bitcoin ETFs has not filled the gap. BlackRock’s iShares Bitcoin Trust (IBIT) bled $2 billion over nine straight days of outflows through Wednesday, and the funds posted a sixth straight day of net outflows on Thursday. Weekend trading is typically thinner, leaving the bitcoin price more exposed to sharp swings in either direction.

Miners got some relief overnight. Bitcoin’s mining difficulty dropped 9.13% at 12:02 a.m. ET Saturday at block 975,744, falling from about 148.36 trillion to 134.82 trillion, the largest cut since June. Difficulty adjusts automatically every 2,016 blocks to keep average block times near 10 minutes.

The next tests for the bitcoin price arrive quickly. Traders will watch whether Saturday’s slide holds below Friday’s $67,555 low, and whether Nvidia’s report on Tuesday extends the chip-stock weakness that has followed bitcoin lower all week. With a December rate hike fully priced and the dollar at a 19-month high, any further jump in yields when markets reopen could keep pressure on.

At the time of writing, the bitcoin price is near $65,100.

Republican Senator Wants to Freeze AI Data Center Boom as Congress Pushes to Expand It

Republican Senator Josh Hawley wants Congress to hit the brakes on new data centers, just as lawmakers are moving forward with legislation intended to make building the infrastructure behind America's AI boom easier.

The Missouri senator called for a nationwide moratorium Wednesday, listing it among five priorities for Senate Republicans during the six weeks before Democrats take control of the chamber.

“Senate Republicans trying to figure out what to do in the next 6 weeks?” Hawley wrote on X. His answer included a moratorium on data centers, alongside defunding Planned Parenthood, suspending the gas tax and other Republican priorities.

A spokesperson told Politico that Hawley wants a federal pause until Congress passes a “bill of rights giving taxpayers guarantees of no rate increases.” Hawley has not presented detailed legislation specifying which projects would stop or how long the freeze would last.

Data centers are the warehouses packed with powerful computers that train and operate services such as ChatGPT. Building more AI systems means installing more servers, which requires additional electricity, cooling equipment and often upgrades to local power grids.

Senate Republicans trying to figure out what to do in the next 6 weeks? Here you go:DEFUND Planned ParenthoodMORATORIUM on data centersSUSPEND the gas tax FUND rural hospitals SAVE AMERICA ACT

— Josh Hawley (@HawleyMO) November 11, 2026

The scale helps explain the concern. U.S. data centers consumed an estimated 183 terawatt-hours of electricity in 2024, according to figures cited by Pew, and that could climb to 426 by 2030. In other words, their annual electricity demand could more than double in six years.

On Monday, the Senate voted 73-25 to advance debate on the Bipartisan American Affordability and Jobs Act, a proposal backed by lawmakers from both parties and President Donald Trump's administration.

The bill would speed federal reviews for energy projects and transmission lines, helping bring new power supplies online. It would also require data center operators to pay the additional grid costs their projects create, rather than shifting those expenses onto ordinary electricity customers.

In practical terms, Congress is considering how to keep construction moving without making households subsidize it. Hawley is asking lawmakers to stop new construction until consumer protections are in place.

His position is not entirely new. In February, Hawley introduced the GRID Act with Democratic Senator Richard Blumenthal, seeking to make new data centers obtain electricity from their own sources rather than relying on existing grid supplies.

He now joins independent Senator Bernie Sanders and Democratic Representative Alexandria Ocasio-Cortez in publicly supporting a national pause. Their moratorium proposal goes further, calling for rules addressing AI's effects on workers and public safety as well as the environment.

The politics have shifted quickly. In the November 3 elections, voters backed restrictions on data center development across the country, including local bans and measures requiring public approval before construction.

A Pew Research Center analysis published Wednesday found 60% of Americans uncomfortable with a new data center in their area, including 54% of Republicans and 68% of Democrats. Another survey found 58% disliked political leaders who support building data centers near them.

Opponents worry about rising bills, water consumption and noise. Cities have already imposed restrictions, while OpenAI recently promised to cover electricity and grid costs at future campuses it owns and operates.

Industry supporters see a bigger economic risk. BlackRock CEO Larry Fink warned last month that a construction moratorium could trigger a recession because so much investment now depends on AI infrastructure.

A federal construction freeze could delay the additional computing capacity that AI companies expect to bring online, along with the power infrastructure built to support it. Existing data centers would not necessarily shut down, but the scope of Hawley's proposed moratorium remains undefined.

Hawley's call does not freeze a single project, and he has not said whether he would support the Sanders bill. For now, the Senate is debating legislation to speed infrastructure approvals, not legislation to ban new data centers.

#5099. Meta tests prediction market app using ‘cred’ points instead of cash: WSJ

Looking for institutional-grade crypto trading? Discover LMAX Digital, the regulated exchange from LMAX Group

Meta’s employee trial advances its prediction market push as Kalshi and Polymarket battle state gambling lawsuits.

Meta has started internal testing of a prediction market app with its employees that would let users compete on guessing the outcome of events without putting real money on the line, The Wall Street Journal reported Friday.

The app, now going by the name Takes, lets users bet "cred" points, according to the report, which cited people familiar with the matter. Similar to other mainstream prediction markets that use money, correct predictions on Takes earn cred while wrong ones lose points.

The testing phase follows June reports that CEO Mark Zuckerberg directed employees to build a standalone prediction market app that would use points, though Meta had not ruled out eventually allowing real-money betting. At the time, the app was going by the name Arena.

The Journal's report does not say whether real-money contracts remain a possibility. Meta declined to comment or give a timetable for a public release.

It comes as real-money platforms like Kalshi and Polymarket are facing lawsuits from states that accuse them of offering illegal gambling. The platforms maintain that their event contracts are legal and fall under the jurisdiction of the federal government.

Speaking at the Cantor Convergence conference on Friday, Commodity and Futures Trading Commission Chair Michael Selig criticized states for pursuing criminal charges against prediction market executives, adding that the disputes should be raised to the Supreme Court.

"Somebody says it's gambling, somebody says it’s a derivative," Selig said. "That's not something that should be at the risk of going to jail."

#5098. Record hedge-fund leverage could soon spill over into Bitcoin

The Fed’s November report puts gross notional leverage at a Q1 2026 record in data starting in 2013. Losses or tighter funding could spread across markets.

Hedge-fund leverage reached a record in Q1 2026 on the gross notional measure, according to the Federal Reserve’s Nov. 13 Financial Stability Report. For Bitcoin, the risk is that losses or demands for additional collateral force leveraged investors to cut positions, spreading pressure across markets.

The record covers the series beginning in 2013. The report also describes reduced Treasury basis-trade activity alongside record or near-record leverage across equity, relative-value and macro strategies. That broader assessment is why a smaller basis trade alone cannot establish that exposure to financial stress has eased. The comprehensive leverage observation is months old; the report generally reflects information available by Oct. 28.

Gross notional leverage compares total exposure, including derivatives, with net asset value. Balance-sheet leverage compares gross assets with net asset value. Neither is a measure of net directional exposure after offsetting positions, so a gross leverage record should not be read as an equally large one-way market bet.

The Treasury basis trade pairs bonds bought with repo borrowing, secured against the bonds, and a short Treasury futures position. Shrinking that trade can reduce one source of financing demand, while other leveraged strategies remain exposed to losses or tighter funding.

Dallas Fed researchers argued on Oct. 16 that modest basis-trade retrenchment could reflect changing relative returns and the appeal of alternatives such as swap-spread trades. Those trades combine Treasury and interest-rate-swap positions. That is a possible explanation for strategy substitution, not proof of how every fund repositioned.

The financing mechanism matters more than the strategy label. The Fed’s stability report warns that material losses or worsening funding can generate spillovers, while higher volatility can constrain dealers’ willingness and ability to intermediate.

Bitcoin’s connection is conditional. IMF research published in January 2022 found stronger Bitcoin-equity co-movement and spillovers during 2020-21 than before the pandemic, with spillovers tending to intensify during market volatility. That historical evidence supports a route for cross-market stress; it does not establish today’s correlation or identify hedge funds currently selling Bitcoin.

The September dealer survey provides a counterweight. Conducted Aug. 11-24 and covering changes from June through August, it found client leverage, broad financing terms and market functioning basically unchanged on net, although some dealers reported wider equity collateral spreads.

Stable conditions can coexist with high leverage: two-thirds of the 15 dealers with at least a decade of hedge-fund business placed leverage above the midpoint of its past-decade range. These were qualitative assessments, not November measurements.

The warning signs to watch are tighter collateral terms and investors cutting positions across markets. Stable funding would weaken that case. A smaller Treasury basis trade alone cannot establish that Bitcoin is insulated from broader deleveraging.

#5091. Congress Summons Ex-Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang, Lutnick Over Chip Exports to China

Congress Summons Ex-Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang, Lutnick Over Chip Exports to China

Sen. Tim Scott (R-SC), chairman of the Senate Banking Committee, has asked former Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang and Commerce Secretary Howard Lutnick to testify, widening a congressional inquiry that has moved from Huang's private life to U.S. controls on chip exports to China. "The American people deserve answers," Scott wrote in a tweet announcing the requests on Thursday evening.

In a letter dated November 12, Scott asked Huang to appear voluntarily at a public hearing on December 3 over public reports that "raise significant national security concerns." The committee has jurisdiction over export controls, "including their application to NVIDIA's exports of advanced semiconductors," he wrote. If Huang does not appear, the committee "is prepared to use all tools at our disposal, including subpoenas."

Recent reports raise serious concerns about our national security and the protection of sensitive U.S. technology.

I am requesting that NVIDIA CEO Jensen Huang and Commerce Secretary Howard Lutnick testify before the Senate Banking Committee.

The American people deserve… pic.twitter.com/Xu1kiNzkhW

— Senator Tim Scott (@SenatorTimScott) November 13, 2026

Scott asked Lutnick to testify on December 10, noting Commerce's "central role in issuing and enforcing export control regulations." A Lutnick spokesperson told NBC News that he had "proactively agreed to appear voluntarily," while the White House said President Donald Trump "maintains complete confidence" in Lutnick.

Separately on Thursday, the Republican chairs of the House Foreign Affairs, China and Intelligence committees, Reps. Brian Mast (R-FL), John Moolenaar (R-MI) and Rick Crawford (R-AR), wrote to Huang, Nvidia General Counsel Tim Teter and Jeffrey Kessler, the Commerce Department's under secretary for industry and security.

Huang was asked to sit for a closed-door, transcribed interview on December 1 and Teter on December 2, with each given until 8 p.m. on Friday to agree or face a subpoena. Kessler, whose bureau administers export controls, was invited to a hearing on December 3.

"As reports continue to come to light regarding Jensen Huang, NVIDIA, and MegaSpeed, there are clear national security concerns that need to be addressed immediately," the three chairs said in a statement. They called on Congress to add four chip security bills to the annual defense authorization bill.

It is their second request. A letter dated November 4 had asked Huang to brief the committees on November 18. Mast told reporters on Thursday that Huang had agreed to brief lawmakers, NBC News reported.

At the center of the inquiries is Megaspeed, a Singapore-based Nvidia customer. U.S. officials suspected the company of helping Chinese firms get around restrictions on advanced AI chips, The New York Times reported in October. Its sister company in Malaysia bought at least $4.8 billion of AI equipment containing Nvidia chips between June 2024 and February 2026, according to trade records cited by the Times.

Megaspeed told the Times it had hired outside counsel to conduct an internal investigation. Nvidia has said it complies with U.S. export laws, according to NBC News.

The Times reported that U.S. officials had concluded that Alice Huang, a former Megaspeed executive, was a Chinese intelligence asset. China has rejected that as a smear, and Attorney General Todd Blanche said on Thursday that the Justice Department had not reached a conclusion on whether she was a spy, according to BBC News.

Jensen Huang admitted on Sunday to being unfaithful to his wife and resigned on Thursday. Nvidia's board has said it has no reason to believe national security or company secrets were compromised.

Moolenaar told reporters that Huang had "been one of the strongest proponents for not having export controls," Roll Call reported. On Wednesday, Sen. Elizabeth Warren (D-MA), the Banking Committee's ranking member, and Sen. Chris Coons (D-DE) asked Lutnick, Secretary of State Marco Rubio and White House Chief of Staff Stephen Miller to preserve records on Nvidia chip exports to China.

China's foreign ministry warned on Friday that tighter U.S. export controls on semiconductors would threaten the stability of global supply chains, Al Jazeera reported.

Nvidia shares closed down 4.3% at $166.19 on Thursday and were up 2.7% at $170.63 in premarket trading early on Friday, according to Yahoo! Finance. The company has lost almost $500 billion in market value since the close on Friday, November 6. It reports quarterly earnings on Tuesday, the first under interim CEO Colette Kress.

#5090. Bitcoin ETFs lose $1 billion for the second week in a row as crypto funds, except one, continue bleeding

Looking for institutional-grade crypto trading? Discover LMAX Digital, the regulated exchange from LMAX Group

The 12 U.S. spot bitcoin funds have lost $1.91 billion over seven straight trading days, and no other crypto ETF categories, save one, saw any net inflows last week.

U.S. spot bitcoin Bitcoin (BTC) ETFs saw $1.07 billion in net outflows last week, their second straight week with more than $1 billion leaving the funds, per The Block's analysis of SoSoValue data. The last time the funds posted back-to-back $1 billion outflow weeks was in late May and early June.

The selling stretched to nearly every crypto ETF category. Of the 15 groups of U.S. spot crypto funds tracked by SoSoValue, 10 posted net outflows last week and four reported no flows at all. Avalanche Avalanche (AVAX) funds were the only category with a weekly inflow, taking in a minute $227,000 on Thursday. Overall, U.S. spot crypto ETFs saw about $1.55 billion in total leave the funds.

The bitcoin funds saw outflows in all five days last week. They lost $201.1 million on Monday, $95 million on Tuesday, $299.9 million on Wednesday, $284 million on Thursday and $185.5 million on Friday.

Combined with the $1.28 billion that left the funds the week before, bitcoin ETFs have now shed $2.35 billion over two weeks. That is the largest two-week total since the fortnight ending June 5, when the funds lost $3.14 billion.

Friday's outflow was the bitcoin funds' seventh consecutive daily loss, a losing streak that has drained $1.91 billion from the funds since it began Nov. 5. It is the longest daily outflow streak since the 10-session stretch from June 17 to July 1, which drained about $2.71 billion, as The Block reported at the time.

BlackRock's IBIT accounted for $143.5 million of Friday's outflows, or about 77% of the total, per SoSoValue. Fidelity's FBTC lost $41.9 million, and the other 10 funds reported no flows. IBIT currently holds bitcoin worth $53.99 billion against $64.19 billion in cumulative inflows since launch.

Bitcoin ETFs have come to lose $2.35 billion in November after nine trading days, already their largest monthly outflow since June, when $4.51 billion left the funds. They are now down about $1.98 billion for 2026, more than double the $914.1 million deficit they carried a week earlier after flipping negative for the year.

The outflows came as bitcoin dropped below $69,000 early Friday, its lowest level since Aug. 20 at the time, as traders weighed Jensen Huang's resignation as Nvidia CEO and expectations of another Federal Reserve rate hike, as The Block reported. The world's most valuable cryptocurrency fell further on Saturday morning, currently trading around $65,650, per The Block's Bitcoin price page.

Spot ether Ethereum (ETH) ETFs lost $398.9 million last week, per SoSoValue, their seventh consecutive negative week; the category has twice posted eight-week outflow streaks, most recently when it matched that record in early July, so another negative week would tie it again.

The seven-week losing streak has pulled about $2.42 billion from the funds since late September, per SoSoValue's data. Monday's $184.5 million outflow was last week's largest, followed by $144.8 million on Wednesday and $70.6 million on Thursday.

A minuscule $2.4 million inflow on Friday ended a six-trading-day outflow streak worth $594.4 million. Fidelity's FETH drew $11.6 million that day, offsetting a $9.3 million outflow from Grayscale's Ethereum Mini Trust, while BlackRock's ETHA reported no flows.

Ether ETFs are now down about $804.5 million for 2026, nearly double the $405.6 million deficit from a week earlier. Net assets fell 15.5% over the week to $10.85 billion, their lowest since Aug. 18 and about $670 million below the funds' $11.52 billion in cumulative net inflows.

Spot Solana ETFs lost $32.1 million last week, per SoSoValue, their sixth straight negative week and the longest such run since the funds launched in October 2025. The outflow streak began the same week the funds snapped a record 14-week inflow run, and it has drained $197.4 million since.

The Solana funds posted outflows in every session last week, extending their daily losing streak to seven days. Fidelity's FSOL accounted for all of Friday's $3.1 million outflow. Net assets fell to $1.14 billion from $1.31 billion a week earlier, though, unlike their BTC and ETH brethren, the funds remain about $644.1 million positive for the year.

Grayscale's Zcash fund, ZCSH, the only U.S. spot Zcash ETF, lost $30.7 million, nearly as much as the nine Solana funds combined, per SoSoValue. It was the fund's second straight negative week, and the outflow matched an early-October week as its largest since late September. ZCSH's net assets fell 32% over the week to $523.7 million.

Spot XRP ETFs shed $19.1 million, per SoSoValue, their largest weekly outflow since mid-March and a second straight negative week. Most of it left on Monday and Tuesday, at $10.4 million and $7.6 million, respectively. On Friday, Grayscale's GXRP lost $5.5 million while Franklin's XRPZ took in $4.5 million.

The three U.S. Hyperliquid ETFs lost $4.3 million, extending their weekly outflow streak to six, the longest since they began trading in May, per SoSoValue. Smaller funds tracking NEAR, Chainlink, Hedera and Polkadot lost between $99,000 and $1.4 million each. The BNB, Dogecoin, Tron and Litecoin funds reported no flows for the week.

The Avalanche funds’ modest $227,000 inflow on Thursday, their only day with any net flows, formed the only black in a sea of red last week.

#5087. Louisiana AG calls CFTC chair an ‘obsequious standard bearer’ for prediction markets following agency lawsuit

Looking for institutional-grade crypto trading? Discover LMAX Digital, the regulated exchange from LMAX Group

The CFTC sued Louisiana on Friday, a day after a state judge ordered Kalshi, Polymarket, Coinbase, Robinhood and dozens of affiliated entities to stop offering event contracts in the state.

Louisiana Attorney General Liz Murrill accused Commodity Futures Trading Commission Chairman Michael Selig of making "flatly false statements" on Saturday, a day after the agency sued the state to block a court order shutting prediction markets out of Louisiana.

"Flatly false statements by the 'obsequious standard bearer' for Kalshi and prediction markets. Gambling has been regulated by states for 250 years," Murrill wrote on X at 12:05 p.m. ET.

Murrill was replying to a Friday evening post in which Selig said event contracts "have traded openly in Louisiana for years, in compliance with the CEA and [CFTC] regulations." He called the state's "eleventh-hour 'emergency' lawsuit" against the industry "naked lawfare and a cynical political stunt."

"Under the Court’s logic, contracts such as interest rate swaps and weather derivatives, both of which are commonly traded financial instruments that the Louisiana economy is highly dependent on, are illegal gambling products," a Kalshi spokesperson told The Block. "Nothing could better illustrate why Congress gave the CFTC exclusive jurisdiction over our national financial markets."

"Prediction markets are regulated by the Commodity Futures Trading Commission under a federal framework, not a patchwork of state rules," a Polymarket spokesperson told The Block.

The CFTC and the United States filed a complaint in the U.S. District Court for the Eastern District of Louisiana on Friday, naming the state, Murrill, the Louisiana Gaming Control Board and the state police's gaming enforcement division as defendants. The agency is asking the court to declare that state gambling laws are preempted for CFTC-regulated markets and to bar Louisiana from enforcing or extending the state court's order.

"The Commission's lawsuit seeks to maintain the status quo while the federal courts decide this issue in an orderly manner," Selig said in a statement. The agency said Louisiana is the 12th state it has sued to defend its claimed exclusive jurisdiction over derivatives markets.

On Thursday, the CFTC and the Justice Department also sued Arkansas to block its enforcement action against Kalshi. Selig told a conference audience on Friday that states were "essentially trying to lock up founders and CEOs" of prediction market companies, The Block reported.

Judge Jeffrey Johnson of the 21st Judicial District Court in Tangipahoa Parish signed the ex parte temporary restraining order Thursday afternoon, according to a copy Murrill posted on X. Murrill sued the companies earlier that day, making Louisiana the 11th state to sue one or more prediction markets.

The order names 31 entities tied to Kalshi, Polymarket, Robinhood, Crypto.com, Coinbase, Gemini, Interactive Brokers' ForecastEx, Novig, Underdog, IG Group and ProphetX. It bars them from offering event contracts on sports, elections, awards and other events to people in Louisiana without a state license, and requires them to geofence the state.

The order expires after 10 days unless the court extends it. A hearing on the state's motion for a preliminary injunction is set for 9 a.m. local time on Nov. 30.

Kalshi has cut off access for Louisiana users. A message in its app, seen in a screenshot reviewed by The Block, tells them, "Your government says you can't use Kalshi anymore," and says the state acted "without a hearing, notice, or due process." The screen includes a link reading "Protect your freedom to trade" and a button to "Join the movement."

The Block has reached out to Polymarket and Murrill's office for comment.

Updated at 4:45 p.m. ET with a statement from Kalshi and at 5:20 p.m. ET with a statement from Polymarket.

#5086. Strategy (MSTR) Adds 485 Bitcoin for $33.4 Million as It Spends $228 Million Buying Back Preferred Stock

Strategy bought another 485 bitcoin last week. A Monday 8-K filing shows the Saylor-led firm spent $33.4 million at an average of $68,887 per coin between November 9 and November 15, lifting total holdings to 850,849 BTC bought for $64.20 billion at an average of $75,452.

The coins were funded with net proceeds from MSTR common stock sales. Strategy sold 2,087,040 shares for $244.7 million, put $33.4 million into bitcoin, used $206.3 million of those proceeds to buy back its STRC preferred and parked $5.0 million in USD Cash. No preferred stock was sold under the ATM during the week.

The STRC buyback was larger than that ATM slice. Strategy retired 2,282,510 STRC shares for $228.1 million, funding the other $21.8 million with interest earned on its cash, cash equivalents and short-term investments. It still has $332.2 million left under its preferred repurchase program and $1.0 billion under its MSTR buyback authorization, with about $18.1 billion of MSTR still available under the ATM.

The $228.1 million preferred repurchase was nearly seven times the $33.4 million bitcoin spend. The 485-coin add was also less than half the 1,036 bitcoin Strategy bought the week before.

BREAKING: Public companies Strategy and Strive bought over $47 million worth of Bitcoin during this dip 💥Strategy: 485 BTCStrive: 251 BTC pic.twitter.com/alNNfxbQY2

The filing lands as the spot bitcoin price sits well below Strategy’s average cost. A week earlier, the same treasury was only modestly below cost after bitcoin slipped under $75,000.

The new coins do not close that gap. They were bought at $68,887, above today’s price but well below last week’s $79,012 average, and they shaved the company’s overall cost basis by only $4, to $75,452 from $75,456.

Strategy now holds more than 4% of bitcoin’s 21 million supply cap. The pace of buying has slowed as the firm has put more ATM cash into preferred buybacks than into bitcoin, a pattern that also marked the prior week’s purchase. Between June 30 and August 10, Strategy sold bitcoin in four consecutive filings to fund preferred dividends and STRC buybacks before flipping back to accumulation.

Strategy used $18.9 million of its USD Reserve during the week to pay preferred dividends. As of November 15, the reserve stood at $4.75 billion and USD Cash at $151.2 million.

On November 15, the company said it will keep the regular dividend rate on STRC at 12.00% for record dates in December. Daily STRC dividends for the month total $1.00 per share. Management said it will recommend keeping that 12% rate until STRC “has demonstrated sustained, healthy trading near $100 per share.”

The board also declared quarterly cash dividends, payable December 31, of $2.50 a share on STRF and STRD, $2.00 on STRK, and €2.50 on STRE. Strategy said it expects those December payments, including the daily STRC dividends, to be treated as non-taxable returns of capital to the extent of a shareholder’s tax basis.

The filing covers a week that ended with a sharp weekend slide in the bitcoin price, a move that also weighed on bitcoin-linked stocks. MSTR last closed Friday at $111.62.

The update comes as Strategy faces index-fund selling after MSCI removed the stock from its USA index, with funds required to sell by November 30. The SEC 8-K is the primary disclosure; Strategy also posts the same bitcoin figures on its public dashboard.

At the time of writing, bitcoin is near $64,100. The test now is whether Strategy keeps buying through a deeper underwater stack or leans even harder on preferred buybacks while the ATM stays open.

#5084. Bitcoin Drops to $69K On 'Concentrated Liquidation' and 'Weakening' Demand

Bitcoin Drops to $69K On 'Concentrated Liquidation' and 'Weakening' Demand

Leading cryptocurrency Bitcoin has dropped 5.6% on the day, and is currently trading at just over $69,300, per Decrypt data. It comes after the latest consumer price index, or CPI, results came in "hotter than expected," experts said, with the drop was triggered by a "concentrated liquidation."

Bitcoin's plunge took it to a 24-hour low of $67,922, its lowest level since mid-August, when it boosted from $63,000.

"Bitcoin’s drop below $69,000 was directly triggered by a concentrated liquidation of leveraged long positions," Tim Sun, senior analyst at Hashkey, told Decrypt. "Though the deeper issue lies in steadily weakening market demand."

Sun highlighted U.S. Bitcoin spot ETFs recording six consecutive days of net outflows, according to CoinGlass. He also pointed to stablecoin supply shrinking and MSCI removing digital asset treasury firm Strategy from its funds.

"ETFs, stablecoin liquidity, and corporate treasuries have served as the primary capital pillars supporting this Bitcoin rally," Sun said. "With all three coming under simultaneous pressure, the market is losing the marginal buying power that previously propelled prices higher."

Meanwhile, other major cryptocurrencies have also taken a hit. Ethereum has dropped 4.6% on the day to $1,953, BNB has slipped 4.7% to $639.46, and Solana is down 3.1% to $80.47, according to Decrypt data. The global crypto market cap has dropped 4.6% over the past 24 hours to $2.39 trillion.

It follows U.S. CPI rising 0.4% in October, which is "hotter than expected, even if the headline monthly rate eased to 0.4% from September’s 0.6%," according to CoinShares head of research James Butterfill. "More troubling was the composition," he added. "Food rose 0.5%, services remained firm, and core CPI increased 0.3%, with services excluding rent, a gauge of domestic price pressure the Fed watches closely, rising 0.5%. The inflation problem is therefore no longer easily dismissed as an oil story."

The consumer price index, or CPI, is a way of measuring inflation that tracks the average price changes of everyday goods and services over time. Central banks tend to use CPI to inform whether they'll cut or hike interest rates, meaning higher-than-expected CPI signals a potential Federal Reserve hike.

Polymarket predictors put a 97% chance on the Fed raising interest rates this year, up from 75% at the start of the week. The CME FedWatch tool, meanwhile, sees an 88% chance of a 25 basis point hike, with 12% anticipating a 50 basis point rise.

On top of the CPI, Bitcoin's downward move comes following Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang resigned from the company, after reports of multiple affairs including with a suspected Chinese spy and a senior company executive spread.

Huang's departure has sparked "severe volatility across tech stocks, further dampening market risk appetite," Sun told Decrypt. "This has prompted broader portfolio de-risking, amplifying the downward pressure on Bitcoin," he added.

For a crypto rebound to happen, Sun said that Bitcoin needs "long-term allocation capital" to flow back into crypto after ETFs, stablecoins, and digital asset treasury companies have taken a hit in recent weeks.

#5082. Kava Lend loses roughly $3.8 million to hack, sending KAVA token down nearly 40%

Looking for institutional-grade crypto trading? Discover LMAX Digital, the regulated exchange from LMAX Group

Onchain data shows an attacker borrowed out nearly all of Kava Lend's KAVA, worth about $3.76 million before the attack, then bridged most of it to BNB Chain and sold it for roughly $2.1 million.

An attacker drained about 55.2 million KAVA tokens from Kava Lend, the native lending protocol of Layer 1 blockchain Kava (KAVA), late Friday after inflating the value of deposits in a dormant stablecoin market and borrowing against them, according to onchain data reviewed by The Block.

The tokens were worth roughly $3.76 million at the $0.068 price KAVA traded at before the attack. Kava Lend's module account, where the protocol's pooled deposits sit, went from holding 55.33 million KAVA to 0.19 KAVA in a little over an hour and a half.

"We are tracking unexpected behavior originating from a dApp within the Kava ecosystem," Kava wrote on X at 6:28 a.m. ET on Saturday in its first public acknowledgment of the incident, more than seven hours after the attack started.

In a follow-up post about half an hour later, Kava said it was aware of "the bridging and sale of KAVA tokens on BSC." "To clarify: This is not an infinite mint," the team wrote. "The event is isolated and contained within Kava Lend."

Kava hasn't put an official number on the losses or published a post-mortem report of the attack.

The exploit ran through Kava Lend's market for BUSD, the Binance-branded stablecoin that issuer Paxos stopped minting in 2023. The market was all but empty ahead of the attack, onchain data shows.

Kava Lend works out what depositors have earned by dividing the interest borrowers pay by the market's net supply, per Kava's public source code. The code skips the division if that supply figure is exactly zero; however, it doesn't check for figures that are minute, but still above zero.

At 11:01 p.m. ET on Friday, the attacker used a small BUSD loan to bring the market's net supply down to two hundred-millionths of a dollar. Over the next three minutes, Kava Lend multiplied the value of every BUSD deposit roughly 243,000-fold. That included BUSD the attacker had deposited ahead of time, which the protocol now counted as roughly 1.25 billion BUSD in collateral.

The attacker then borrowed KAVA against their deposit 127 times, using a second account to clear each loan and get around Kava Lend's cap on KAVA borrowing. The attacker appears to have prepared for at least a week, running repeated deposit and borrow cycles in the BUSD market from Nov. 8.

Beginning at 4:59 a.m. ET on Saturday, the attacker bridged about 49.2 million KAVA to an address on BNB Chain through Kava's LayerZero bridge. Most of it was sold within the hour for 3,006.5 BNB and 231,557 USDT, worth about $2.09 million combined, and the BNB has since been moved out. Another roughly 4.58 million KAVA went to five unlabeled addresses on Kava.

KAVA was trading near $0.042 at time of publication, down about 39% over the past 24 hours, according to The Block's Kava Price page. Partner exchanges paused deposits and withdrawals "as a standard precautionary measure," Kava said.

At 9:05 a.m. ET, Kava said it had paused the Axelar bridge "out of an abundance of caution," though the drained KAVA left the network through LayerZero. "We will share a complete incident report as soon as it is ready," the team wrote.

The Kava incident follows several lending protocol exploits this year where an attacker borrowed against collateral worth far less than the protocol believed. Cronos halted its network in August after an exploit of lending protocol Tectonic estimated at $75 million, and Hedera's Bonzo Lend was hit for roughly $9 million in July.

#5081. Bitcoin Price Trades Near $64,000 After Weekend Plunge to $62,000

Bitcoin price traded near $64,000 on Monday morning, down about 2% from Sunday, according to Bitcoin Magazine Pro data. The world’s largest cryptocurrency is trying to hold its ground after a weekend plunge to $62,000, as tech stocks slid ahead of Nvidia’s earnings and traders braced for a Federal Reserve rate hike next month.

The selling that broke Friday’s low on Saturday morning did not stop there. Bitcoin slipped under $64,000 Saturday afternoon and dropped below $62,000 in the 8 p.m. ET hour on some exchanges, its lowest level since July 14. It bounced back above $64,800 within the hour and reached about $66,100 early Sunday before sellers returned. The bitcoin price has spent most of the past day between $64,000 and $65,000.

The slide has been punishing for leveraged traders. About $2 billion of crypto positions were liquidated over a 24-hour period during the weekend, the vast majority of them long bets. Bitcoin is now down 17% in November and has posted six straight weekly losses.

Stock futures offered little help on Monday. Contracts on the Nasdaq 100 dropped 1.5% and S&P 500 futures slipped 0.7%, while Nvidia fell 3% in premarket trading. Investors weighed a Financial Times report that former CEO Jensen Huang and his allies are discussing a campaign to reinstate him, a week after his resignation sent bitcoin below $72,000.

Nvidia reports third-quarter results on Tuesday, and options markets imply an earnings-day move of about 7% in either direction, Pepperstone’s Chris Weston told Reuters. Bitcoin has traded in step with chip stocks for much of the past week, so a weak report could add to the pressure.

Rates remain the other weight on the bitcoin price. Traders fully price a quarter-point hike at the Fed’s December meeting, with odds of a half-point move near 8%, CME FedWatch data show. The 10-year Treasury yield eased to about 5.12% on Monday after jumping 29 basis points last week, and higher yields have lifted the dollar to its strongest level since April 2025. Fed Chair Kevin Warsh speaks in San Francisco later Monday, and minutes from the last Fed meeting are due Wednesday.

Bitcoin-linked stocks fell with the coin. Strategy (MSTR) was down 8% in premarket trading and Strive (ASST) fell 9%. Strategy is also facing index-fund selling after MSCI removed it from its USA index, forcing index funds to sell by Nov. 30.

Spot bitcoin ETFs shed another $185 million on Friday, their seventh straight day of outflows, bringing losses since Nov. 5 to about $1.91 billion. That run follows BlackRock’s iShares Bitcoin Trust (IBIT) bleeding $2 billion over nine straight sessions.

Analysts are now focused on the $60,000 level, which marked the lows of February and June. John Roque, technical strategist at 22V Research, said bitcoin is close to completing a head-and-shoulders top. “Bitcoin has not broken below its neckline at 60,000,” he wrote in a note Saturday, adding that such a breach “would then imply risk to $38,000.”

Matt Maley, chief market strategist at Miller Tabak + Co., wrote that bitcoin “NEEDS to hold the $60k level,” warning that “any material break below that level would be quite bearish.” A gauge of bitcoin’s 30-day implied volatility climbed to 49.75 over the weekend, its highest since June.

With Nvidia’s report, Fed speakers and the Fed minutes all due in the next three days, the bitcoin price faces a busy week. A move back above $66,000 would recover Sunday’s bounce high, while a break below the weekend low near $62,000 would put the $60,000 floor in play.

At the time of writing, the bitcoin price is near $64,100.

#5079. Traders claim $6 million profit by spotting BitGo’s public pull request and frontrunning memecoin listing

Looking for institutional-grade crypto trading? Discover LMAX Digital, the regulated exchange from LMAX Group

A pseudonymous trader claims to have anticipated a memecoin's Robinhood listing, which sent its price soaring, by tracking code changes in BitGo's public repository and Wintermute smart contracts.

A pseudonymous trader said their group profited more than $6 million by buying two Robinhood Chain memecoins ahead of their Robinhood listings after spotting a public BitGo code change and analyzing Wintermute's onchain activity.

The trader, who posts on X as @3i, described their "edge" in an article published on Saturday that has drawn more than 300,000 views. The Block confirmed several of the public records the trader cites and found wallet activity that matches parts of the timeline, though could not verify the profit figures.

Robinhood launched Robinhood Chain, a Layer 2 network built on Arbitrum's tech stack, on July 1. BitGo announced wallet and custody support for the network the same day, while Robinhood has named algorithmic trading firm Wintermute as one of its largest crypto market makers in an SEC filing.

According to 3i, the pair began tracking Wintermute's wallets on Robinhood Chain and BitGo's activity on GitHub after it noticed that Wintermute had been moving tokens to Robinhood about an hour before earlier memecoin listings were announced.

Indeed, a BitGo developer had opened a pull request, or a proposed code change, in the company's public BitGoJS repository on July 27. Included in the code: "Add hoodeth:cashcat (Cash Cat) token to hoodethTokens.ts on the Robinhood Chain." The change was merged the next day.

While Cash Cat was launched on July 1, Robinhood didn't list the token until Aug. 6, ten days after the July 27 pull request was made.

BitGo released a new version of its code on July 29, which included the Cash Cat code change alongside a separate change that added support for 99 Robinhood Chain stock tokens. "The implications of this were huge, as it confirmed my hunch that Robinhood was the one behind this request," 3i wrote. "Why else would cashcat be in there next to their native stock tokens?"

Though 3i said the pair had been buying up the memecoin in the days after the pull request, eventually accumulating 1.5% of the token's overall supply, they had been waiting for a stronger signal to buy even more: Wintermute whitelisting the token, or transferring the token to Robinhood.

The trader claims that on Aug. 3 at 1:48 p.m. ET, their monitoring bot caught Wintermute approving the trading pool. "That was the alert we’d been waiting for," 3i wrote. "The desk had selected a venue to trade the exact coin we were holding."

The pair began buying slowly to avoid drawing attention to their wallets, 3i wrote. But about half an hour later, at 2:19 p.m. ET, a wallet the pair suspected belonged to a Robinhood insider placed an $11,000 limit order for the memecoin. Knowing that other traders were watching the same wallet, the pair abandoned the slow approach and bought more than $1 million worth of Cash Cat at once, bringing their holdings to around 4% of the token's supply, according to 3i.

Per onchain data reviewed by The Block, a wallet made its first Cash Cat purchase at 1:54 p.m. ET on Aug. 3, six minutes after the Wintermute alert 3i describes. After three small buys, the wallet bought about 12 million tokens in two swaps at 2:20 p.m. ET, one minute after the suspected insider's order, before moving the tokens to other wallets. In total, it picked up about 15 million tokens, or 1.5% of the supply.

Onchain analytics firm Bubblemaps flagged the wallet the next morning, noting that it had spent $1.1 million on the memecoin as part of $2.7 million in purchases by a cluster of fresh wallets. While 3i's article claims ownership of the wallets in question, The Block could not independently confirm who controls them.

The Block also identified a Wintermute-labeled contract on Robinhood Chain that keeps a public list of tokens it is approved to trade, which may be one of the three contracts 3i describes that carry that list. However, Cash Cat was not added to that list until around 6:31 a.m. ET on Aug. 5, nearly two days after the signal 3i claims to have caught, but still about 27 hours ahead of Robinhood's listing announcement.

Robinhood announced that Cash Cat was available to trade at 9:31 a.m. ET on Aug. 6, three days after the pair's largest purchase. 3i wrote that while they napped through the token's initial price spike following the listing, the pair still made more than $4 million between them by selling into the frenzy.

3i also claims the pair managed to get ahead of Robinhood employees who were themselves trading the listing. Another pseudonymous trader, clickless, published a separate account on the day of the listing describing a wallet they believed belonged to an insider, which placed a Cash Cat limit order before the announcement and ultimately lost money.

3i said the pair later repeated the trade with PONS, the token of a memecoin launchpad on Robinhood Chain. The trader said they began buying on Aug. 28 and that they and "friends" made $2 million, though the article does not explain how they identified the token.

What looked like a Sept. 2 listing of PONS, however, appeared to have been "some kind of honeypot for Robinhood to catch the insiders," 3i wrote. The trader added that most of the advantage described in the article has since faded.

On Sept. 15, the Department of Justice charged two former Robinhood engineers with commodities fraud and wire fraud for allegedly trading perpetual futures on Hyperliquid ahead of Robinhood listing announcements. Robinhood said at the time that it had investigated the matter and reported it to law enforcement.

The Block has reached out to Robinhood, Wintermute, BitGo and @3i for comment.

#5074. Nvidia Employees Push Board to Bring Back Jensen Huang: Report

Nvidia Employees Push Board to Bring Back Jensen Huang: Report

Some Nvidia employees are calling on the company's board to bring back Jensen Huang, days after he resigned as CEO following reports of extramarital relationships, according to internal correspondence reviewed by Wired.

An internal "suggestion box" post, submitted by a longtime engineer prior to Huang's resignation, urged the board to take employee sentiment into account before acting, Wired reported. It has since drawn more than 1,600 comments, several of which asked the board to find a way to reinstate Huang. "If you believe in the mission, bring back THE boss," one anonymous commenter wrote.

The post also called on the board to share the findings of its investigation with staff and to formally seek their views on Huang continuing as CEO. One employee went further, sending a companywide email demanding transparency about Huang's exit and declaring: "I stand by Jensen."

In the hours following Huang's departure, some two dozen Nvidia employees posted supportive messages on LinkedIn, while Wired said it could not find any employee posts backing his departure. The engineer behind the suggestion box post declined to comment, and Nvidia and its new board chair, Harvey Jones, did not immediately respond to the outlet.

Huang resigned as Nvidia's president, CEO and a board member on Thursday, ending a 33-year tenure at the company he co-founded. Chief financial officer Colette Kress is serving as interim CEO, and Jones, a former CEO of Synopsys, became the board's first chair. Nvidia did not give a reason for the resignation.

Huang told staff on Sunday that he had been "unfaithful" to his wife, without naming anyone.

The New York Times had reported in October that surveillance of Alice Huang, also known as Huang Le, indicated that she had developed a "close relationship" with the Nvidia CEO. Per the Times, U.S. officials concluded she was a Chinese intelligence asset, while the Financial Times reported that a U.S. memo said the two had been romantically involved. China has denied that she is a spy.

Alice Huang was CEO and chair of Megaspeed, a Singapore-based Nvidia customer, and was investigated by Singapore police over possible false declarations relating to the company's server purchases, the country's Ministry of Home Affairs said on Thursday. She was not investigated for espionage, it said.

Police found insufficient evidence that she had committed any offenses, but concluded she had knowingly given them inaccurate information to downplay her role at Megaspeed, according to the ministry. She was issued a stern warning, left Singapore in February and was barred from returning. Investigations into Megaspeed and six other people are ongoing.

Alice Huang is now living in Beijing with the knowledge of security officials, Bloomberg reported, citing a person familiar with the matter. Attorney General Todd Blanche said on Thursday that the Justice Department had not yet concluded whether Huang was a spy, and that the FBI was investigating the reports, according to Reuters, which was unable to reach her for comment.

On Wednesday, the Times reported that Jensen Huang had also had a relationship with Kimberly Powell, Nvidia's vice president of health care, who reported directly to him. Powell has not commented.

On Thursday, Reuters reported, citing two people familiar with the matter, that Huang had an intimate relationship with a second Nvidia employee, whom it did not name. Nvidia declined to comment to Reuters, and Huang and a representative for the board did not respond. Huang has not commented on the reports since his Sunday email, according to Wired.

The calls inside Nvidia echo criticism of the board's decision from tech and crypto figures. Binance co-founder Changpeng "CZ" Zhao called it a mistake for Nvidia to let Huang resign, while Elon Musk said hiring him would be "an honor."

Huang still faces scrutiny in Washington. Congressional committees have asked him to sit for a closed-door interview on December 1 and to testify at a Senate hearing on December 3, as lawmakers examine Nvidia's chip exports to China.

He and Nvidia also face a proposed shareholder class action over his alleged ties to Alice Huang.

Nvidia reports quarterly earnings on Tuesday, the first under Kress.

#5070. Sweden’s H100 Taps Coinbase Prime and Deribit for Its 3,506 Bitcoin Treasury as It Courts Family Offices

Swedish bitcoin treasury company H100 Group said Monday it will use Coinbase Prime and the Deribit derivatives exchange for custody, trading and financing of its roughly 3,506 BTC, a move it says supports both its bitcoin-per-share goal and a push to serve wealthy investors.

H100, which trades on Stockholm’s NGM Nordic SME under the ticker H100, announced the partnership in a release Monday morning. The company and its subsidiaries will use Coinbase and Deribit, which Coinbase owns, for custody, trading and financing, it said.

H100 valued its holdings at about SEK 2.3 billion, or $226 million, based on a bitcoin price of $64,600 on Nov. 15. It also has about SEK 219 million ($22 million) in outstanding convertible loans that carry 0% interest, mature on July 9, 2030, and equal less than 10% of the stated value of its bitcoin.

The release did not disclose fees, collateral terms or borrowing limits. It also didn’t say whether H100 has drawn on any financing, how much of its bitcoin will sit with Coinbase, or whether it is moving coins from another custodian.

H100 described Coinbase’s offering as the crypto version of a bank’s prime brokerage desk, bringing custody, financing and trade execution under one roof.

The trading itself will be run in-house. PDI AS, a wholly owned trading and technology unit, builds the software and trading systems used to manage H100’s bitcoin, and the company said those tools operate “with protecting H100’s Bitcoin holdings as the first priority.”

“This partnership gives H100 stronger tools for our own balance sheet, and we use them on our own Bitcoin first,” Executive Chairman Sander Andersen said. “Bitcoin per share remains the measure we run the Company by, and these tools are there to help us deliver on it, not to add risk.”

Andersen added that this is “the first of several” partnerships, “and the next is already under way.” He did not name the next partner.

Lauren Abendschein, head of institutional sales at Coinbase, said bitcoin treasury companies “have grown into an established part of our institutional client base,” and that their priorities are custody, “liquidity when the market moves, and financing against the same balance sheet.”

The second goal is bigger. H100 said it wants to become what majority shareholder Geir Harald Hansen, in a June 11 letter to shareholders, called “a leading European alternative and digital asset manager.” It will start with family offices and high-net-worth individuals looking for a partner on bitcoin investments and financing, and said it will share details as products launch.

H100 began as a health-tech company before adopting a bitcoin treasury strategy in 2025. It agreed in January to buy Swiss treasury firm Future Holdings and closed that deal in February.

Its holdings tripled in August. After shareholders approved the acquisition of two Norwegian holding companies in June, H100 closed the deal on Aug. 10, adding 2,455.37 BTC and lifting its total to 3,506.4 BTC, according to its second-quarter report.

H100 paid for that deal with 790.5 million new shares. That took its share count to about 1.13 billion and left Hansen with about 69.2% of the company. Eirik Grøttum, who has worked with Hansen for years, became H100’s CEO the next day.

Grøttum wrote in the same report that simply raising money to buy bitcoin “is unlikely to be sufficient on its own” for treasury companies, adding that “BTC should be the hurdle rate, not the business model.”

The announcement comes as bitcoin trades near $64,000 after a weekend slide to its lowest level since July, and treasury companies face pressure to show they can grow their bitcoin per share without issuing too much stock. H100 has not said whether it plans to buy more bitcoin.

#5067. CoW Swap goes live on Solana with intent-based trading

Looking for institutional-grade crypto trading? Discover LMAX Digital, the regulated exchange from LMAX Group

Solana lending protocol Kamino is integrating with CoW Protocol at launch.

CoW Protocol CoW Protocol (COW) has launched an alpha version of its decentralized exchange aggregator, CoW Swap, that settles trades directly on Solana, initially supporting swaps between USDC and wrapped SOL.

CoW Swap has offered swaps on Ethereum since 2021. It also supported cross-chain swaps from Ethereum-compatible networks into Solana before the alpha, which now lets users start and finish a swap directly on the Layer 1. According to CoW DAO, the decentralized organization behind the platform, the protocol has processed a total of $219 billion in trades.

Solana apps that integrate the protocol can route users' orders to more than 30 solvers, which can match orders directly or draw on liquidity onchain or offchain, CoW DAO said in a statement shared with The Block. Kamino, one of Solana's largest lending protocols, is integrating with CoW Protocol at launch. Kamino first unveiled its own swap feature in December 2024 at The Block's Emergence conference.

CoW DAO argued its Solana launch helps address two problems for traders on the network: failed swaps and sandwich attacks. Swap trades can fail if the price moves beyond the level a trader is willing to accept, but still costs them a network fee. In a sandwich attack, a bot trades just before and after a user's swap, profiting at the user's expense.

"Solana traders are tired of failed trades and sandwich attacks," CoW DAO co-founder Anna George said in the statement. "CoW Protocol has spent five years solving exactly that on Ethereum, and now we're bringing it to Solana."

On CoW Swap, traders sign an order, called an intent, that specifies what they want to trade and the price they'll accept. CoW covers the transaction fee and puts up the SOL deposit for account rent for sponsored orders. Independent firms or teams called solvers compete to fill those orders, and if no solver can meet a trader's price, the order can expire without a failed swap going onchain. Solvers group the trades they can fill into batches and settle them together on Solana, making it harder for bots to insert their own transactions immediately before and after an individual swap, Cow DAO said.

"When solvers compete to find better prices on Solana, Solana users win," CoW Swap VP of Growth Alex Marsh said.

CoW DAO said it will waive its volume fee on Solana for at least 12 months after launch.

#5066. Hong Kong to License Crypto Dealers and Custodians by Year-End

Hong Kong will introduce a bill before the end of the year to bring crypto dealers, advisers, asset managers and custodians under a licensing regime, Financial Secretary Paul Chan said Monday, even as the city has approved only two of 36 first-round applicants to issue stablecoins.

Chan delivered the remarks in a keynote at the Bloomberg Global Regulatory Forum in Hong Kong. “Before the end of this year, we will introduce a bill to complete the regulatory perimeter, extending licensing to those who deal in, advise on, manage and act as custodians of digital assets,” he said.

The plan would amend the Anti-Money Laundering and Counter-Terrorist Financing Ordinance, expanding a regime that now covers virtual asset trading platforms, the government told the Legislative Council on Nov. 4.

“On stablecoins, we have moved forward step by step. Of the 36 applicants in the first round, two received licences in April, and both have since launched Hong Kong dollar stablecoins,” Chan said. He said the emphasis is on “genuine use cases and credible business models,” citing cross-border payments as an example.

The Stablecoins Ordinance took effect in August 2025. The Hong Kong Monetary Authority granted the two issuer licenses this April, the government said in that earlier Legislative Council reply. Chan did not name the two issuers or say when a further application round might open.

The low approval rate is the point. Hong Kong is trying to build a regulated digital-asset market without opening the door to every issuer that applies.

“Our principle is simple: same activity, same risks, same regulation,” Chan said. On tokenisation, he said funds offered to the public in Hong Kong have grown about seven-fold in a year, and that since last month two tokenised money market funds have traded around the clock on a licensed platform. “Our approach is consistent: the same requirements as for conventional products, with additional safeguards for the new risks.”

Those figures line up with a fuller count the government gave on Nov. 4. As of the end of September, the Securities and Futures Commission had authorized 17 retail tokenised products, including 13 tokenised money market funds, with about HK$10.3 billion in assets in their tokenised classes. Two of those money market funds began round-the-clock secondary trading on licensed platforms on Oct. 26.

“Above all, we are firm on one point: Decentralisation does not mean less accountability,” Chan said.

Hong Kong already licenses virtual asset trading platforms. The new bill is meant to pull dealing, advisory, management and custody work into the same ordinance, a step the government has framed as streamlining the legal perimeter rather than writing a new one from scratch.

Acting Secretary for Financial Services and the Treasury Joseph Chan told lawmakers on Nov. 4 that international platforms may apply for a license if they meet the statutory tests and the securities regulator’s rules, and that the coming bill is intended to let a wider range of service providers operate in the city. The government did not give a date for when the bill would be introduced, beyond “by the end of this year,” and did not say how long lawmakers would have to debate it.

Hong Kong has spent recent years trying to position itself as a regional hub for digital assets, even as other Asian financial centers have rolled out their own licensing regimes. Chan’s speech did not mention bitcoin by name, but the firms that deal in, advise on, manage and hold bitcoin in the city would fall under the expanded perimeter once the bill becomes law.

If the bill reaches the Legislative Council before year-end, dealers and custodians that have so far sat outside the platform-licensing regime will have to decide whether to apply, or to stay out of Hong Kong.

📉 Crypto Sells Off as Jensen Huang Exits Nvidia

Two weeks ago, The New York Times reported that Nvidia’s CEO had a close relationship with an executive at a Singapore chip buyer under US export-control investigation. Things escalated quickly from there.

On Sunday, Jensen Huang told employees he had been unfaithful to his wife of 40 years, and Nvidia’s board opened an investigation into whether he had been targeted by a foreign intelligence operation. On Wednesday, The Times reported a second relationship, this one with an executive who reports directly to him. Three Republican committee chairs were preparing a subpoena.

Many believe that the Binance post-hack aftermath has been weighing on crypto prices, and now perhaps this coverage will help alleviate some pain.

And then Nvidia reports fiscal Q3 results on Tuesday. Any positive news about Jensen succession planning, the Q3 results and/or future growth will likely stop the bleeding on one of the biggest assets across markets (also helping crypto most likely).

We will find out soon if it’s enough to stop the bleeding…

#5062. ‘Roadmap is Complete,' Cardano Founder Charles Hoskinson Says

‘Roadmap is Complete,' Cardano Founder Charles Hoskinson Says

Charles Hoskinson said that the Cardano "roadmap is complete" and that everything he said would be built has finished. It came during a YouTube livestream Saturday where Hoskinson sparred with disgruntled investors as ADA sits down 94% from its 2021 high, per Decrypt data.

One viewer, named shevilkid1738, pushed Hoskinson's buttons by claiming he'd promised Cardano would overtake Bitcoin. The Cardano founder clapped back, saying there was no evidence he ever said that.

"I never promised that. I said I'd love for it to happen, and I believe that Cardano is a better store of value than Bitcoin is for a variety of reasons," Hoskinson said. "I don't promise price appreciation, or people overturning things. I say it's absolutely possible."

Shevilkid1738 responded over an hour later with a quote where Hoskinson said he "thinks" Cardano is "probably" going to become the biggest cryptocurrency in the world. Hoskinson highlighted the word think and said that he has "taken it as far as I can," explaining that the network is now decentralized and doesn't rely on him as a founder.

The viewer wasn't happy and called it "insane" for a founder to no longer have a responsibility to those who invested in their project.

"Under what conditions does that obligation end?" Hoskinson said, "The roadmap is complete. Everything I said is going to be built has been built. It reached four times the value of Honda. There's millions of people that have used it, and millions of people continue to use it. It is self-determining and self-governing, with its ability to pick and choose its own leaders without my consent."

Cardano’s roadmap was split into five eras: Byron, Shelly, Goguen, Basho, and Voltaire.

Byron launched the network in September 2017, Shelley added staking in July 2020, and Goguen brought smart contracts via the Alonzo hard fork in September 2021. Basho, the scaling era, came via the Vasil upgrade in September 2022, before the Chang hard fork kicked off Voltaire, the governance era, in September 2024.

The final piece to the puzzle occurred in January 2025, when the Plomin hard fork gave ADA holders "real voting power" over the blockchain’s future. This marked the transition to "full decentralized governance," and apparently the end of the final roadmap era.

"The goal was simple. There's a roadmap. Byron, Basho, Shelley, et cetera, et cetera, and it's done," Hoskinson said. "I have no power over this ecosystem."

Ultimately, though, Hoskinson says the issue that investors have is financial.

"You want me to pump your bags. That's what you're really saying. The job's not done till it's pumped," he said. "But guess what? It's never done then, because somebody comes in at $3 ADA, and then expects it to be five. And then somebody comes in at $5 ADA and expects it to be seven. If you retire at five, you've defrauded the person at five. If you retire at three, you've defrauded the person at three. There's no winning."

ADA hit an all-time high of $3.09 in 2021 and has been on a downward slide ever since. By 2023, it hit a low of $0.25 before pumping 384% to a high of $1.21 by the end of 2024. Since then, its price has tanked 86% to $0.17.

"Apparently, I have to go pump your bags, and if I don't, I'm a scammer," Hoskinson said. "Would you sign up for that deal? Would any human being sign up for that? I thought slavery was made illegal."

#5061. Vitalik Buterin backs higher gas costs for creating accounts and deploying smart contracts to help Ethereum scale

Looking for institutional-grade crypto trading? Discover LMAX Digital, the regulated exchange from LMAX Group

The proposed gas changes could break existing applications, leaving developers to address compatibility issues before Glamsterdam.

Ethereum Ethereum (ETH) co-founder Vitalik Buterin is backing a proposal that would make creating accounts, storage slots, and smart contracts on the network more expensive than it currently is.

In an article he shared Monday, Buterin defended EIP-8037, a proposal planned for Ethereum’s upcoming Glamsterdam upgrade, saying the network cannot scale up transaction capacity without limiting the growth of data that nodes must store.

Buterin explained that execution capacity can expand much faster than Ethereum's state, but he said allowing both to grow at the same rate would overwhelm node operators and make it harder to maintain a decentralized network.

Buterin argued that lowering gas costs for other operations would be more disruptive than raising the cost of creating new state.

"The goal of EIP-8037 is to decouple Ethereum’s scaling limits: enable a much higher amount of execution (which is easy to scale), but only a little bit more state size growth (which is hard to scale)," he wrote.

Proposal EIP-8037 would raise the gas required to create a new account from 36,600 to 197,900 gas units, in Buterin's example. Users would also spend 110,020 units, up from 22,100, to create a new storage slot and nearly 37.8 million gas units, compared with 4.95 million currently, to deploy a 24 KiB smart contract.

Notably, the proposed account-creation charge would also apply when an ETH transfer creates a new account onchain.

Buterin said Ethereum currently adds about 110 GiB of state annually, but his illustrative estimate put that figure at 1,100 GiB per year if the block gas limit rose tenfold without repricing state creation. According to Buterin, this would put additional pressure on disk storage, initial synchronization, and state-related operations.

Buterin also acknowledged that EIP-8037 could break existing applications, including bridges with hardcoded gas limits. Developers will need to review their contracts and transaction configurations before Glamsterdam, he said.

#5059. Upbit parent Dunamu posts $14 million Q3 net loss as revenue falls 61%

Looking for institutional-grade crypto trading? Discover LMAX Digital, the regulated exchange from LMAX Group

Meanwhile, Dunamu is currently in the process of a merger-acquisition with South Korean heavyweight Naver Financial via a stock exchange.

Dunamu, the parent company of South Korean crypto exchange Upbit, swung to a net loss in the third quarter as a broad downturn in digital asset markets weighed on trading activity and revenue.

According to its official disclosure, Dunamu posted an operating loss of 3.5 billion won ($2.6 million) and a net loss of 18.6 billion won ($13.8 million) for the third quarter. Both figures represented a shift to losses from operating profit of 235.3 billion won ($174.3 million) and net profit of 239.0 billion won ($177 million) in the same period last year.

It reported consolidated operating revenue of 151.7 billion won ($112.4 million) for the quarter, down 60.7% from 385.9 billion won ($285.9 million) last year.

“The deterioration in sales and profitability is attributed to the contraction in global digital asset market trading, weaker investor sentiment, and operating expense burdens,” the company said in a translated statement. About 96% of Dunamu's revenue comes from the Upbit trading platform.

Dunamu's overall performance so far in 2026 also shows a notable decline from the previous year.

For the nine months ended Sept. 30, 2026, consolidated operating revenue totaled 559.8 billion won ($414.7 million), down 52.9% from 1.19 trillion won ($880 million) in the prior-year period.

Operating profit for the nine months was 108.0 billion won ($80 million), down 86.2% from 784.8 billion won ($581.3 million) a year earlier, while net profit was 89.8 billion won ($66.5 million), down 86.3% from 657.8 billion won ($487.3 million).

Meanwhile, Dunamu is currently in the process of a merger-acquisition with South Korean heavyweight Naver Financial via a stock exchange. When closed, Dunamu is set to become a subsidiary of Naver Financial, and the combined entity plans to aim for a public listing — potentially on Nasdaq — within five years.

While the transaction faced delays due to regulatory headwinds, the country's Fair Trade Commission has reportedly started a review of the deal, nearly a year after the companies filed the merger notification, signaling potential progress.
